I 4 .

LADIES' LETTER.

El DEAR NELLIE,

I am reminded that I have not spoken of capes lately. lam certainly reminded of that fact by the coolness of the summer evenings. The Lady contains a few specimens of fashionable capes. I notice that the present fashion of transparent yokes and insertions renders light wraps absolutely necessary whenever the wind is cool, and these must be very dainty and smart, to accord with summer attire. Black is proverbially the most useful colouring, and when relieved with some white silk or chiffon, and some 6ortA or antique hued lace, the effect is smart and chartiing.

Hats of the most fashionable type are picture hats. I really think that the name conjures up to the imaginative of my friends a view of wild and cultivated flowers with very fine trimmings all placed on a big brimmed hat. The picturesque hat demands the hair being dressed somewhat looser and more freely, but even then it must not be allowed to degenerate into caricature by those who would be in good style. The weird heads of hair seen amongst the second-class followers of fashion in imitation of defunct Parisian and Ameri- can modes are not to be seen at any gathering of the better class. For good style now the hair is dressed broadly, and just over the top of the ears, waved and worn with but little " fringe " (as we have known it time back), only a few soft curls just to take off the severe effect,, and in many eases where the hair grows closely and softly round the forehead, no definite curls even.

I have seen a lovely black alpaca travelling coat, which doubtless invited the dust more freely than would a grey alpaca, but at the same time it was so much more elegant that it deserved to be for- given that little weakness. It was crowned with a very small soft chiffon toque, also in black, with a large chou of white 134136 ribbon velvet in the front, and the fresh appearance of the traveller was to be enhanced at the moment of arrival by the adjustment of a new white tulle bow round the neck. The dress worn beneath this ulster con- sisted of a white pongee shirt, with a transparent yoke of lace, and a black alpaca skirt lined with white silk. The same costume would have looked admirable had the skirt and long ulster been of a thin light grey covert coating, or, as I said before, of grey alpaca.

Cloth and serge skirts are still made without the smallest fulness just below tho waist. When they are well-cut the folds arising naturally from the circular sweep of the material, there is nothing ugly or ungraceful about them. But many are not well out, and they drag on the figure. I sincerely hope that a little fulness may be again the fashion before lang, for it is not everyone who can afford to employ a skilled tailor or dressmaker, and the unskilled make a fearsome thing of the tightly. fitting skirt, even in substantial stuffs. In muslins and washing silks the results are even worse.

_ Trissores, linens, hollands, and piques are being packed by the score for country and seaside, now that all the world is off to ruralise. White hats, white gloves, white stockings, and white shoes, are again this season the fit and proper etceteras, and with a radiantly white gown, not long enough to trail, they make "the summer girl" look her very best, whether she walks, wheele rows, drives her pony-cart, or sas listening to the band with a volume of her favourite author in her hand. I am reminding those of my friends who are about to take their holiday abroad that white gowns are a necessity—there is always much white worn over the water. This year it will be white crepe de chine, white silk muslin, and white voile. Of the last material I have seen a charming gown made with the skirt, which points to the hem in the front and has a triple effect at the back, bear- ing incrustations of raised silken flowers. The bodice had a V-shaped top, and was folded round the figure to the waist beneath a narrow belt of pale blue velvet, fastened with a diamkand slide. This belt being the only touch of color was ex- tremely effective, and by reason of its narrow width extremely becoming to the waist. Waist are becoming very slim, I notice; is it the exercise in which we indulge, is it tight lacing, or is it a marvellous result achieved by woman out of mere respect for fashion ? Indeed, the close. fitting skirts and dresses seem to demand a slim figure, and, somehow, whatever fashion ordains, woman arranges to-see that she gets it. At least that is the opinion of

Your loving JENNIE.

McDOUGAL PATENT SELF-RAISING FLOUR MAKES THB Br ST PASTRY.

===== 0002601_18990825_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
.‘ WOMEN'S CHAT."

The majority of the elite are at present taking their "cure" and Marienbad is, undoubtedly, owing to the I visit of the Prince of Wales, the most fashionable resort I this year. Here early rising is the order of tee day, six o'clock being the hour at which the first glass from the springs should be partaken of. Then comes breakfast which is usually had out of doors in the woods,at eleven the bathe,after which comes the second glass of mineral water. Lunch is served from one teetwo. Everyone has tea in the woods, then CDITles the third and last glass of the water, and dinner at 7-30. At ten o'clock those sho rigidly follow out the "cure," have retired to rest. So far as dress and gaiety are concerned many of the recognised resorts in Switzerland to-day closely resemble the London Season in full swing. Up to a year or two ago the great attraction was the simplicity of the life. One found good air, good food, early hours, moderate prices, and simple dress. But all this is changed now. Lucerne, for example, is at the present time, crammed, and dances, dinner parties, picnics, concerts, theatricals ecc., are the regular routine. Rest, quiet. simple dress- ing, and simple living, are not to be tolerated in these nineteenth century end days of ours, and we have not time to admire glorious views, and enjoy grand scenery. It appears prebable that Madame Patti will give a couple of concerts in Berlin this coming winter, her fee for tbe same being £1,200. !The figures are high for this particular capital, but the diva has frequently received more. Madame Melba is not now very far behind. She can command four hur.dred guineas for an engage- ment, and it is whispered that her terms will shortly be further raised. Tbis, as before, is in the hope that the sum will prove prohibitive, and so give the Australian nightingale the chance of a much needed rest. The sixty-ninth anniversary of the birthday of the Austrian Emperor, celebrated the other day, completed a year which.has been sadly eventful to his Majesty. It was on September 10th that the whole civilised world was shocked by tbe dastardly murder of the Empress, and by the irony of fate there fell in the same year the fiftieth anniversary of the Emperors coronation—an occasion which is commcnly regarded as one for jubiiation and festivities. Despite the terrible bereavement of less than three months before—not the first unhappily that has overtaken the Emperor-King—his subjects were scarcely deterred from some demonstration of their loyalty, and indeed it is no matter for surprise that they should have wished to take the opportunity of display- ing their affection and sympathy towards a mcnarch who has teen so sorely tried. Of some afflictions one way bay in the beautiful words of Virgil "Farsan et teec ohm meminisse juvabit" but the misfortunes which have almost overwhelmed the Royal House of Austria have been so awful as to leave little room for consolation. At the same time the British people join with his Majesty's subjects in the hope that it may please Providence to prolong a life upon which much of the future of both Austria and Hungary depends. Scarcely a week passes without the publication of some book which illustrates the universal belief in a future state. A new edition of Mr. Poth's work on the Tasmanians, and Sir Alfred Lyall's Asiatic essays both refer to this subject, and describe the attitude of undoes, and the now extinct people of Tasmania towards the spirits of the dead, which in both cases havo been supposed to maintain relations with the living. Sir A. Lyall tells a strange story which is indicative of the superstition of the Elindoos. Some fifty years ago a very high English official died in a fortress, and at that moment when the news of his death reached the sepoy guard at the main gate, a black cat rushed out. The guard presented arms to the cat as a salute to the flying spirit of the powerful Englishman, and the conviction took so firm a hold of the locality, neither exhortation nor orders were effective to prevent a Hindoo sentry at the gate from presenting arms to any cat that passed out of the fort at night. Judging from the number of low broad trunks en evidence women are at length realising that this method of disposing of one's toilettes etc., when travelling, is vastly superior to the old fashioned idea of cramming all one's bats and divers description of clothes into a Noah's Ark. A Noah's Ark is an abomination and ought to be put down by Act of Parliament. It means chasing porters, pursuing cabmen, making enemies of servants and dependants, told off to attend to one's luggage, and alienating 01301 friends. A woman who had lately received a visit from a girl, and a Noah's Ark, was relating to me her experiences the other day. The girl arrived, and the cabman promptly refused to tackle the luggage single handed. The gardener was summened, as sbe keeps no men in the house,and half way up the back staircase the "Ark" stuck. It would neither go up nor come down. After considerable damage had been done to the wall, it came down, and commenced its ascent of the principal stair- case. Here it knocked down a valuable picture, and at the conclusion of the visit, on its career downstairs, left scratches innumerable, and in some cases irremedi- able on the lovely oaken stairs and landings. That girl is never likely to rrceive a further invitation to that particular house. But, to return to my subject, the flat broad trunk. No porter or cabman shirks the charge of it, it will go up or come down any staircase, and will slip under the railway seat and form a delightful footstool. It is sur- prising too, the number of articles it will hold, provided one keeps them level as they go on, boots and shoes, then underwear, shirts, and bodices last. it is a thing of amazing amiability. One of the new hat-boxes will hold all headgear without fear of crushing, and with a neat hold-all for umbrellas, sunshades, and en-tout-as, and another for wraps, one can sally forth, strong in

connection that one stands very little chance of proving a terrcr to others or oneself.

Black evening gowns are one mass of embroidery. The most fashionable show bold scroll-like works, and con- ventional flowers, in jet paillates laid over one another like the scales of a serpent. Blue steel with which much ot this work was enlivened, is in great favour, and its effect at night is brilliant. CURRIED SARDINES.—Take three tablespoonfuls of fresh olive oil put it in a saucepan, thicken it to a sauce with arrowroot previously mixed smooth with cold water, add a small dessert spoonful o f curry powder, stir well, and simmer for five minutes, adding a little chutney if liked. Skin as many sardines as required, taking care not to break them, lay them gently in the muce. let them get thoroughly hot, and serve on toast with the sauce poured over. .

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 25, 1899

(Copyright.) THE

REVENGE OF VALERIE:'

A ROMANCE OF BRITISH COLUMBIA.

BY HUME NISBET,

Author of "A. Sweet Sinner," "Bail Up," "The Rebel Chief," "Kings of the Sea," "Hunting for Gold," "The Swampers," &c.

lie stood betore tier in tits usual serious and attentive manner. She did not look at him. but at the map opposite her on the wall, while Silb fumbled nervously with his unopened letters. What she had come to say did not seem so easy to begin as she had anticipated. She had come fully provided against the cold, and was clad in heavy sealskins. Outside a keen north wind was blowing, making people shiver and double up as they faced it, but in this office it was almost oppressively warm. At least, she felt as if her sealskin oppressed her. She longed to cast it aside, yet she resisted the temptation. All at once her dark eyes roving from the map, fell abruptly on the table. She felt that although he was gazing at her respectfully, yet he was watching her keenly, and she resented this from such a base tool. It made her task all the more hateful to have to be familiar and confidential with such a man.

Stephen Belwood waited for her to begin the con- versation, and while he did so he read the cause of her embarrassment. She had come to ask him to do some more dirty work. She was not satisfied with the evil that had been wrought. "Surely it cannot be murder that the jealous beauty is meditating upon this time," he thought as he looked at her so respectfully. "I must draw the line at that, for I have no intention of risking the fatal noose even to please such a good customer. I doubt though if anything much short will please my lady." Suddenly the large heading of the open prospectus caught her glance, and she lifted it up and began to read through it. It would do to break the ice. "Are you going to issue this prospectus, Belwood ? "

"Yes, your Grace, when it has been properly re- constructed."

"I see you are quoting freely from the vendor's reports, but you say nothing about your special commissioner's, Sir Lewis Eastlake's, opinion. I have had letters also from him on these claims, and they were not encouraging." "Pardon me, your grace. Sir Lewis's examina- tion was a cnrsory one. He listened to outside opinions, and did not wait long enough to test these claims properly. Now since his departure the vendors have discovered fresh pockets, and have washed out over sixty thousand pounds worth of gold, which, with what they had stored up before, makes over one hundred and fifty thousand pounds washed out in less than eight months." " Do you believe this to be true? "I have the utmost reason to believe it to be true in every particular. I also believe that our next mail from the Yukon will inform us that Sir Lewis Eastlake has discovered a valuable property in the North East."

"He is safe and alive you think? It is many months since I heard from him last," asked and said the Duchess eagerly. "Yes; he left Dawson with one of the most experienced miners on the field. They 'ad a good store of provisions, and I 'ave since forwarded fresh supplies. Oh, yes, he will be safe, and I hope successfuL " "I hope so, and I shall soon know for myself, for I have made up my mind to take a yacht voyage round America and live at Dawson as soon as traffic has once more opened." Stephen Belwood looked at the Duchess with genuine admiration. He replied: "I wish I could go with you, for lam positive that this will be the best speculation I have ever been in. I have already despatched agents, as you know, to Van- couver, Wrangel and Dyea. My intentions have been to charter a small steamship and send her laden with plant by the same route. This you may be sure I would not do if I wasn't sure of my ground." "I believe you Belwood, and as we both get the name of being lucky lam ready to prove may faith by going in with you to the extent of, say, a hundred thousand pounds." She uttered these words carelessly, as if this sum was a mere bagatelle to her, as indeed, it was. Her listener also received it coAly, yet a little colour crept into his white cheeks and a kind of light into his filmy eyes. "Thanks your Grace. I must now raise the capital from four to six hundred thousand pounds, otherwise we shall have the B.P. reviling us for selfishness."

The Duchess laughed slightly at this sally, then she said kindly: "I shall make you my representative and broker in this speculation, and pledge myself to give you the earliest intelligence about the fields. You may trust my acumen in this matter. If I instruct you to withdraw, if to purchase, don't hesitate." "I can assure your Grace that I trust your busi- ness faculties and hinstincts more than I would many, even of our smartest city men. I will obey your orders to the letter." He spoke with evident sincerity. If there was one woman whom he sincerely admired, it was the young Duchess Valerie; other men were apt to follow blindly in his footsteps, even as he followed her. Her business gifts and unvaried success made him condone her weakness where this blundering baronet was concerned. He had his weaknesses also, and like her, did not resist. "Good," she said. "In that case I shall leave you a cheque for this amount. If I say buy, I'll send you an order on my banker for the amount I wish to spend at the same time. Meantime I have some money to pay you for that what you did at Cherbourg for me, and another favour to ask from you." "Now for the other side of the cameo," thought Stephen Belwood as he bent his head and prepared to listen.

party I can aepena nupon. ne win neip us to Ha the felony charge, but it can't be done for lees than fifty thousand. It might even cost more." _

"Bah f Let it cost a hundred thousand, so that it is done quickly," cried Valerie impetuously. Stephen Belwood rose with a resolute air. "Very well, your Grace; it shall be done, and satisfactorily. In a fortnight from now I'll under- take to have Nurse Eastlake convicted of theft and sentenced to six month" at least, imprisonment, with as bad a record as can be produced against her, only I don't exactly know how we are to get her to own that she is guilty, either of the felony, or of being unfaithful to her 'usband. It is one thing to drag the 'orse to the pond and another to make 'er drink."

"Leave that part of the programme to me to manage," answered the Duchess rising. "She does not know that I am in England, and she still regards me as her friend. You secure the con- viction, with the had record as to her moral char- acter. I shall visit her in prison and shew her the damage she will do to her husband and child if she does not release him from his bondage. I fancy I can play upon her feelings." The rest of the interview was occupied in com- paring notes, and the Duchess signing a cheque and present expenses. Then she rose to depart. am staying at the Hotel Cecil for the time I am in London. If you wish to report progress or require me, wire there and I shall come to you. I hold this business above all others until it is finished."

She did not take his hand, but she bent grace- fully as she passed out to her carriage, which speedily rolled away, leaving Stephen Belwood to his reflections.

As she passed out of the office Dickens, hia secretary, came in. He looked at that seakskin muffled bit of perfumery and soft loveliness and sighed.

-"Ah, these ladies!" he murmured. "What will they not do to have a dip into the City pie ? " Mr. Stephen Belwood, although so defective in the rudiments of his native language, was like Napoleon the Great, a splendid reader of char- acter. His father had made his fortune in the pawnshops and money-lending business. But he had not the broad and daring speculative abilities of his son. Content with sure and legitimate profits he had fought shy of that delirious mael- strom, the Stock Exchange. Like a certain Bishop, Belwood pere had seen no necessity for the know- ledge of grammar for his son, and Stephen was the result of this singular and mediteval cult. He believed, like the said progressive clerical, in facing only the actualities of life, i. e. , in his case, the knowledge of book-keeping and a good judg- ment in the art of valuing property. Belwood, senior, had found these accomplish- ments quite enough for his earthly wants there- fore why shouldn't they also serve for his son? He had not seen the necessity of keeping up a large establishment for the amusement of other people. A couple or so of rooms for the household purposes in one of his pawn-shops had been quite enough. As for trading in the Bull and Bear Market, he eschewed it as he did all other forms of gambling. If the fathers of children could be certain that only their own proclivities and aspira- tions would be transmitted to their offspring, then the learned and right reverend Bishop would be right in his desire to abolish all the elegant branches from public schools, and permit only the children of gentlemen to acquire those accomplish- ments. But as Nature has proved in so many instances, such as with Shakspere, Burns, Carlyle and the great host of illustrious sons of the lower classes, that neither the sires nor the Bishop can foretell what each pupil may become. We trust, therefore, that such sentiments will be received at their proper valuation, as abominable re-echoes from the dark ages. Stephen Belwood had chosen his secretary to supply his own deficiencies. In Mr. Richard Dickens he had secured a man specially adapted for the duties for which he was engaged. He was cultured, gentlemanly and faithful to the secrets of his chief, as far as his employer revealed himself. The chief knew exactly how much to reveal to his secretary. He would not, for instance, ask the assistance of this honourable gentleman in such shady transactions as this private business of the Duchess Valerie. This he intended to manage for himself. Mr. Richard Dickens also did not know anything about those businesses from which so much income was derived. He believed his chief to be concerned alone in the Stock Exchange. He also believed implicitly in his chief as a straightforward and honest man, according to the accepted code of the Stock Exchange. Of course, there are codes and codes of morality. The founder of the Goldenring family, when he followed the British Army to Waterloo and know- ing that Bonaparte was defeated, spread about the market that the British had lost the battle, might not have been accepted as a moral man by rigid advocates of truth or probity. But as he paid his calls when they were due, he was much respected in the commercial world. If a robber, or a pirate acts fairly to his partners, he is called an honest fellow by them. Richard Dickens would have repudiated his chief, had he suspected him of doing anything dishonour- able. Dicky, however, liked a game of poker, and therefore saw nothing wrong in keeping back certain private tips in order to secure a coup. It was only legitimate gambling. He had read the reports of Sir Lewis Eastlake on the Klondike mines, and admired his chief for his punctiliousness in hold- ing back his company from the public so long. He would not have blamed him if he had floated it, even with these discouraging reports. According to the venders, a great deal of gold had already been taken out of the test holes, and that was basis enough to start any company without being accused of fraud.

With the later reports, however, he considered his chief lacking in his usual enterprise in not coming forward sooner. They held the properties, and although, as yet, they had no independent reports, still they had a surer basis to work on than most companies had. The game of buying and selling shares does not depend upon the stock behind. All depends upon how the market is worked. People invest their money purely as a gamble. They purchase and sell, without a thought of what is behind. A company formed on the pur- chasing of land in Mars or Venus would have as much chance of being taken up by the British public, as this one formed on the purchase of gold mines in Klondike. Seldom had Stephen Belwood promoted a company on such a sure basis as this present one, therefore, Dickens seeing his chief grow dilatory about this, formed his own con- clusions. He sold as many shares as he could in other directions, and resolved to put all his spare cash into this one.

That day they spent together, reconstructing the prospectus. When Dicky heard that the Duchess Valerie had plunged so largely he was sure of his ground. He even remonstrated with his chief about issuing a prospectus at all. Why give the public such a splendid chance? "We have six months before us to work the affair," remarked his chief quietly. "I don't feel like risking too much of my own money until I ham quite sure. It will be easy to spread disparaging acounts about when we are sure it is hall right. Let's boom it now."

The prospectus was completed that night, and the company advertised in the papers the next day. They opened their list at ten o'clock and closed it the same night for London and the following day for the country. The preference shares they allotted to themselves, and the ordinary shares to the public. There was a wild rush to the bank appointed to take in the advances on the day the list was open. When the lists closed, they had sold the entire number of shares which they had to dispose, and had now their capital made up. It takes months to get ten or twenty pounds in for a charitable pur- pose. A couple of days are sufficient for the British public to shell out five or six hundred thousand pounds, where such generals as Stephen Belwood take the affair in hand.

The board of directors met on the appointed days and had the most expensive of lunches. There was 47 business to discuss because they were waiting. The first dividend would not be declared for the text eight months at least. Meantime a portion of the capital was expended in fitting out a steamship which had been already secured, and packing off engineers, workmen and plant. They did not spare expenses. There was no necessity. The secretary of Stephen Belwood superintended all the lunches and City banquet& He was a socially-inclined gourmand, and liked this Dart of the programme, which his chief did not. Stephen Belwood's habits were known. He did not shine either at conversation or at a feed. He sat through them gravely and sileitly, lifting the champagne-glass to his lips and seffing it down un- tasted. After these City convivialities he wonld drive home to his palatial residence, to find hie attractive wife entertaining a crowd of fashionable and millionaire guests. He passed through hie aorffeously furnished rooms. with the niseternienes

of modern art on tne waits cuosen tor mm by nil upholsterers, with a bashful air and a wan emits for the guests of his wife. Then, as soon as he tould, he would seek his own study, and there hayip a quiet cup of tea, while he marked down his day'll winnings in his private log hook. He had no friends, and no confidants. ITtE splendid-looking wife was as much a strangpr to him and his secrets as his secretary was. There was no nursery in that sumptuous house. He log a lone man, without principle, and liberated from all illusions.

He had been left money and the instructions to increase it from his father, and this alone stir ported him, for Nature had deprived him of the power of enjoying the good things of life. He vlso a physical wreck, without even the solace of self- respect. ' His vices gave him no pleasure. He spent* good deal on these vices, but he did not enjoi them.

Only one vice remained which gave him all satisfaction, and that he indulged in nightly. FBI pipe of opium. When his day's labour was over, he would prepare and smoke this drug until he fell asleep on the sofa. Then, when the :house was still, he would wak. up, crawl to his solitary bedroom, take his sleep° ing draught, and thus find oblivion. So passed the life of this solitary money spinner.

(To be continued.)

CHAPTER XIII.

lITEPHEN BELWOOD RECEIVES THE DUCHESS VALERIE AT

Ms CITY OFFICE.

Mr. Stephen Belwood was the proprietor of many branches of the financial profession. He also carried on these different branches under so many varied names, that it was extremely difficult to say whether he had any more right to that of Stephen Belwood than he had to the others.

In his City office and clubs, also about the Stock Exchange he was known as Mr. Stephen Belwood, company promoter and capitalist. He was a well- known figure in the City, and as a City man con- siderably respected; for he was keen and sure in his forecasts, and whatever he took up were considered to be sound investments.

He was chairman or director of a great number of companies about which there could be very little doubt as to their solidity, and genuine claims to being what they were represented. In fact, it had come to be taken for granted that the name of Stephen Belwood on the board was a sufficient guaranty for the respectability of that company. If stock was mentioned shareholders could depend upon what was printed being there. He met his calls ayith unvarying punctuality, therefore he was, according to Stock Exchange morality, a highly-respectable citizen. He was also generally successful in extracting gold from what- ever schemes he took in hand, and able to get out after the profits were extracted, without raising the suspicions of those braying watchdogs, the financial paper critics. People who dabble in shares ought to keep their eyes as closely as possible on the leaders and manipulators of the market. Other- wise they have no right to grumble if they have to 'pay instead of being paid. It is all a game of !speculation and duplicity. The City offices of this astute man of business were spacious and furnished regardless of expense. Doors, partitions, counters, tables, and other wood- work were of the most massive and solid polished mahogany, all richly carved. The windows were of the finest and strongest plate-glass, while the brasswork, plates, door handles and hin„ges were ornate, heavy, and kept in the highest state of lustre.

Every modern and commercial appliance was here at hand, while his clerks were all young and active men in the most approved condition of dudism. It appeared an easy and languid business, this company promoting, to anyone entering the outer ,office. The young dandies never appeared to be 'doing any harder work than reading the financial newspapers, picking their teeth, or trimming their nails. As a rule they were much more important and smug than their modest and somewhat wearied-looking chief. Occasionally, as these languid and fashionably- dressed young gentlemen sat on the morocco-covered, hair-stuffed chairs so gracefully killing, or appear- ing to kill, time, a visitor of the same order would drop in and shake hands. Then after a confidential whisper, they would either frown and nod, or else smile gently and nod. Sometimes one would rise, and putting on his silk hat, go forth with his visitor, to a neigh- bouring restaurant, where other Stock Exchange loungers were assembled. A bottle of champagne of extra quality would be ordered, and its merits discussed with other mysterious news. The contents of a goodly number of glasses of wine or cognac passed within those collars and fancy vests during the day, yet these young speculators did not appear to lose their mental balance or run short of cash at any time. True, they took their turn at the bar by relays, yet the news which they whispered must have required a deal of moisture. They invariably returned to the office, as sleek, smug and sedate as ever, no matter how often duty called them forth.

Doubtless these young gentlemen were of service to Mr. Stephen Belwood. as he was not the man to keep them for amusement. Older men when they have to drink so much are apt to get livery; then their consciences begin to prick them, and they find it difficult to lie with the misleading fluency of an innocent and fresh young Throgmortonite, who has not yet worn the coating off his stomach. In the West End Mr. Stephen Belwood had offices, not so sumptuously furnished as his City ones, yet substantially fitted also. There were the offices of "The Concord Discount and Mortgage Company." There was no Stephen Belwood on the brass plate of this estlblishment, but instead, in one corner, the name of the general manager, Walter Gordon.

He was modest about commencing his connection with this most lucrative business. No one except his employers knew that he was the entire com- pany. A few unfortunates, like Sir Lewis Eastlake, understood that he was connected with this firm in some way. They asked for his influence sometimes, and thought him a good fellow when they had their request granted. They considered him merely a shareholder, who sometimes went out of his way to oblige a friend. Partners he may have had, of course; very few capitalists would have carried on such an extensive money-lending business as this without having to borrow themselves. If so, however, they were even more completely hidden than he was from the general public of borrowers. In this business the clerk did not wear superfine attire, nor consume champagne and time in private bars. They dressed plainly, and from the hour they arrived in the morning till leaving off time at night they seldom lifted their heads from their ledgers. He had various other businesses, each of which bred money at the rate of twenty-five per cent, which were conducted by dlfferent managers and under various names. Over each signboard hung the significant three golden balls, that denote the poor man's bank. When visiting his West End establishment and his numerous pawnshops, Stephen Belwood dressed in his shabbiest overcoat and wore a slouch hat, which pretty well disguised him. He on these occasions slunk into the apartments of his managers by private ways, and conducted his business with great secrecy. Only his confidential assistants ever saw him on these occasions. To the ordinary shop- men and clerks he was an unknown personage. He owned a magnificent house in one of the fashionable suburbs, where his wife, a lady of more than ordinary attractions, dispensed her hospitalities on an extravagant scale. These entertainments furnished matter for the halfpenny papers, and kept up his reputation in the City. A company promoter must go in for ostentation and parade if he wishes to succeed.

But although the master of many menials, he never lost his abject and wearied manner. He slunk into his own mansion as he did into his City office, as if he had no real business in either of the places. While his clerks and guests feasted like aldermen, he contented himself with a nibble of biscuit and a glass of water. He seemed to have only one passion, and that was to make money. On the fourth day of January this remarkable man entered his City office fully an hour before his usual time. As he passed through the outer apart- ment, he nodded gravely to his subordinates and said apologetically: "I am expecting a lady here in a few moments. Do not keep her waiting when she comes, but shew her in at once." He went into his own sanctum, the walls of which were covered with maps and sections of maps. Those portions were the properties in which he had an interest were located, were specially coloured. On the table were piles of letters, papers and prospectuses. These he turned over with but little appearance of interest. One of the prospec- tuses, however, he took up and read through care- fully, being a proof which had just come from the printers. It was the prosectus of the "Klondike Moose Mining Company," which had not yet been issued to the public. He drew his pencil through the word "Klon- dike," and put " Yukon " instead. Then he went over to the latest published map of Alaska and the North West Territory and studied it closely. "Dickens is far too cautious and non-committing in his statements," he murmured reflectively. "We shall never strike fire with a prospectus like this. I'must 'aye it hall haltered.—Ah Good-morning to your grace." "Morning, Belwood," answered the Duchess Valerie, as she sailed forward, and took the seat, which he offered her with a bow. "I suppose we have perfect privacy here, Belwood ? No curtain listeners, or anything of that kind ? " "Rest assured habout that your grace; my ois'oesay 15 ricotta- solid."

CHAPTER XIV. A DIABOLICAL PLOT.

The Duchess Valerie, under the compliments and evident admiration of this renowned Stock Ex- change magnate, had recovered her courage. She became gracious and condescending, and even went so far as to open her seal-skin jacket and display an exquisite toilet underneath. A beautiful woman she was. One that few men could have looked upon, without yielding to the spell. Her present companion knew, however, that the only admiration she would receive from him, was the tribute which he had already paid to her mental qualities. He was well aware how utterly freezingly she would have crushed and snubbed him, had he dared to compliment her upon her personal charms. Knowing this he schooled him- self to be discreet.

"I must compliment you," she began suavely, "on the adroit manner in which you conducted affairs at Cherbourg. They could not possibly have been better managed." "I am 'appy to have pleased your Grace—so far," he replied quietly. "Yes, so jar, you have done admirably. But there is more yet to accomplish before I can depart satisfied for Klondyke. You can depend on your manager, Gordon, to carry out his contract, I trust ? "

"The liberality with which your Grace has treated him already, through me, with the promise of future independence, has made him a most devoted slave. Besides, lie tells me he has become sincerely attached to the girl, and will marry her after the divorce." _ _

The DucheFs frowned darkly, while a savage glare leaped from her fierce eyes. "No, I do not desire this sacrifice from him." She burst into a bitter laugh as she added: "Of course, if he wants Hope Eastlake, after she has had a term in prison as a convict, he may please himself. Yet I would prefer that ho leaves the country for Australia after he has given his evidence, and without her." "Do you wish her to be publicly arrested and charged with some crime—say, theft, for instance ? " He asked this question gravely, as if it were a matter of ordinary business. "Yes, and as soon as possible," answered the Duchess fiercely. "Can you manage this for me? I want to make it impossible for her ever to live with her husband again. I wish to separate her from her child and make her glad to say she is guilty for the sake of that brat. In fact, I must have her confession to take out to Sir Lewis East- lake with such clear evidence from outside authori- ties, that he will not pause to sue for a divorce. Do you comprehend me? Can you do this? " He pondered seriously for some minutes, sitting down opposite her and resting his chin on his palm, while he stared in front of him. Then he said:

"Yes, I think hit can be done." "Think?" repeated the Duchess scornfully. "Well, then, hit can be done, if you are williqg to pay the price ? " "I am willing, no matter ho % much it costa. What will it cost to accomplish all this within the next three weeks ? "

A TIMELY WARNING.

I had pains in icy back and in my head, and when I went to 0 doctor he told tne I had a very weak tun. Whether I had eaten anything or nor, I had pains in my chest. The proppsed Campbell v. Gardner match for the blindfold championship seems no nearer an arrange- ment than at first. In last Saturday's " Herald Gardner replies to Campbell's contention for one game at a time only, and points out that in the lat- ter's match with Mclielvie, in 1888, it was agreed to contest six games, simultaneously, out of a total of 28 Gardner gives a review in extenso of this match, and some curious points are brought to light. Finally, Gardner asks the pertinent question—" Will Mr. Campbell explain why he is so reluctant to play simultaneously? THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 25, 1899.

DUTTON HALL. A. UNIQUE AND INTERESTING RELIC.

Within a comparatively easy distance of Li ver- pool, about midway between Runcorn and North- wic'n, standing a little back from the main road betw-en those towns, on a gentle eminence com- manding a lovely prospect of the valley of the Weaver, stands Dutton flail, a flue fragment of six- teenth cen3ury architecture.which in many respects is unique The east quadrangle of the hall or manor now only is standing, presenting a semewhat irregular front of black and white, having four pointed gables in timber and plaster, each differing in size and pitch of roof. The bleck oak transverse beams forming the upper elevation of the porch are richly and elaborately carved with fanciful arabesque devices enclosing the letters "SPDK" and " P J " tied together with a true lovers' knot. Immediately over the doorway, boldly carved in Old English characters, we read the legen I ;—"Syr Peyre Dutto3, Knight, L3rde of Dutton, and my lathe dame Julian, dc wiff,made this hall and buvld- ing in the yere of our Lord God a MCCL:COXIAJ, Who thanketh Go lof all." Immediately behind,in- side the perch,but on the main wall of the building is a portion of another carved inscription, " who thanketh God of all . . . . XXXIX.." pointing to the conclusion that the building of the hall pre- ceded the addition and erection of its porch by three years. The outer doorway of the timbered porch is an adaptation of the Tudor arch, having weE-defined mouldings and pilasters, with cppitals of carved human heads, while the overhang- ing transverse beam forming the cornice is supported by a grotesque of the gargoyle type on one side, and a human head on the other. The door within the porsh, app trently the original one, is several feet in width, and of dart oak, studded with nails and hung with wrought iron hinges, extending its whole wilth, terminating in flew- le-is. Its inner fastening is a haze wooden bolt. In the centre of the door is a smaller one, also similarly studded, hav- ing a wrought iron ring latch, this smaller door being intended as the usual and more carefully guarded entrance. The large panel door is divided into s:x ver- tical panels, the upper portion of each terminating in geemettic ornarnentatioe, above which are smaller panels similarly treated. The two centre panels contain some ancient stained glass. Over the door is a fine pair of spreading antlers are displayed, and on each side of the door are carved woolen pilasters, each surmeunted by a heraldic shield on which are emblazoned the arms ef Dutton and Hatton quarterly, these again being rur- mounted by a helmet and crest having two griphons, parted per fesse, Or and Azure, as supporters. On the pilasters are other carved figures holding a garter and a union rose. Under one shield is a handsome carved wreath encircling the sacred monogram," 1.F1.5;" while under the other is a curious carving representative of the Crown of Thorns encircling other devices symbolic of the five wounds of Cnrist• On the right hand of the entrance hall opened out the buttery and other offices, as in college halls. This portion is evidently of very early date, and c sntaies the main dpyrrooms, whie on the left of the entranee hall is the great hall, forty feet long by twenty feet wide. This once noble apartment was open from floor to ro and was divided from the entrance hall by a screen having pilasters with carved oetaeonal capitals, each apparently consisting of a tree trunk, rising from base to ceiling. Other similar pilasters run up the sides of the hall at regular distances, supporting a coved ceilingeand running all round its fri-ze is an inscriptioe carved in ornamental black letter, detailins the peculiar circum- stances which tee building of this handsome apartment was intended to commemorate. On one side of the hall is a huge fireplace, originally capacious enough in which to roast the traditional ox, and at each side is a cup- board, in which was stored the logs to feed the huge fire Owing to the adaptation of Dutton Hell as a farm- stead,the magnificent ball has undergone material al tera- tiors,the front walls having been taken out and rep!acPd with brick, though they are coloured in panels and bands of black and white, in accord with the more primitive structure, and somewhat mean windows have taken the place of tlsose originally giving light to the hall. Floors have been put in so as to form two storeys, and intersecting walls divide the upper portions in several apertments, almost entirely obscur- ing its fine and unique architectural features. These alterations have broeen up the carved ornamental in- scripture running roiled the covered ceiline into fra- mentary portions,qius making it exceedingly difficult to decipher. Of the history of the "Lerdas of Dutton " much might be written. The fortunes of the family have been closely ant winel with the history of the county, and its con- nection with the national life of the count:.7 since the days of the Nermen Conquest, it not from earlier Saxon times, but as this sketch is only intended to have especial reference to the " hall an 1 buyldyi tee" all else must perforce be omitted, except so far as it is neces- sary and incident thereto. Lawrence Dutton, the seventeenth .Lord of Dutton in succession from the Concoest, was but two years of age on the deatheof his father, Roger, in the year 1476. He was of a retiring disposition, unlike many of his ances- tors in not intertnedclline in the national or political issues of the period. He was ef the estate of an esquire, and spent his time in superintending and exteneing the family estate. He married a daughter of Robert Ducken- field of Duckenfield, in Cheshire. He had a fairly numerous family of sons and daughters, all of whom are statei to have been illegitimate, though in his will he made a special provision of five hundred marks as a marriage dowry for his two " mulier doughtours." As was n-,t infrequently the custom, all his sons and daugh- ters seem to have been included in the home circle, and between him and his son John there was evidently a close bond of warm affection, intimate buiiness rela- tions, and implieit confidence. Lawrence, a year or two before his death, knowing that his son John could not succeed him in the possesQion of the family estates, made special provision for him by deed of gift, convey- ing to him "the rnessuage in Preston nigh Dsresbury, called the New Mannor, for his life, and also his estate at Ouston, and all his purchased lands in Cheshire." As further showing ifs implicit trust in his integrity of character he appointed John his execu- or, though this office carried with it the sad and onerons duty of venting the hall and estate of Dutton in" Sir Pews Dutton, Knyght, as next ryght heyer male, in default of issue, as appereth by my old eve,- dences humbly beseeching him for tree Recta love he hatch born unto me in liff, that it please hvm to see this my present will in all things performed." His body he direc- ted to be" enterred amongs other ray ancestoures in the chapel of our blessed Lady within the monastery of Norton." John did not Iye to see the ball and estate vested in Sir Piers, far immediately a fierce contention arose anrnest the now numerous branches of the Dutton family as to whether the estate was an entailed estate in the main line only. John's death occurring some time before the terminatien of the dispute, his own life estate passed to Sir Piers in the final settlement. Sir Piers at once entered into possession and occupa- tion of the ancestral Hall of the Duttons, and in jubilant commemoration at once set about its renova- tion and enlargement, building the unique apart- ment described in the earlier portion of this sketch as an addition to the old hall " adioyn- ing it unto the domestic chapel, and so making it as one continued building, before which time the old hause stood a little distance from the chappel aforesaid." On the completion of the new hall, he carved under the edge of the coved c-iling in ornamental character s run- ning all round the hall, the record of his victory. "In the thirtieth yeare of lays most gracious majesty this hall and all the chambers adj titling were male and fin- ished anno domini 1539 by the special . . . of Syr Peyrs Dutton, Knight.and dame Julian, his will . . . after long suite made by the heiress general of the Dut- tons against Sir Piers Dutton then of Hatton, Knight. and now owner of this house and Hatton, both depend- ing continually before all the nobility of the King's Most Honourable Council and all the judges of this realme. By the space of seven years an! more the same Sir Piers was approved heyree male and right inhert- tour ot this house and all Dutton lands, and so adjudged by the right honourable award of the most famous Prince King Henry the VIII. under his broad male." As the richly-ornamented porch, which is so unique an en- trance to the hall, bears the date 1512, it is evident that it was added subsequently. In the light of the procei- ing.it is at least a somewhat remarkable fact that n 1536, about twelve months after the final settlement of the e Act of Parliament Sir Hers should be fa°lle nut dt° nb actively engaged in carrying out tile King's c m - mends in the dissolution of the Cheshire monast especially in the spoliation of the "A.bbie of Norton," as recorded by Sir Peter Leycester, although such work would almQst of necessity involve the desecration N Sir Lawrence's tomb, placed there a short nine year's be- fore, and from whose will he was supposei to derive his title to Dutton Hall and eatataa. Another local historian thus writes :—" The progress of the dissolu- tion of the Cheshire menas'eri ,s appears to have been almost wholly confided to Sir Piers Dutton, a profligate minion of the Court, and to subordinate agents nearly as unprincipled, and the most unjustifiable intrigue was reorted to in procerine the surrender of the abbey lands, and the mist wasteful prilusion in the regrant- ing of them." Advantage was taken of the cemnletion of the hall in 1539 to celebrate with due local pomp and pride a double domestic felicity in the farndy of Sir Piers —'hat of the marriage of two of his daughters. From the Conquest to the year 1656, a period of 600 years, a Dutton succeeded a Dutton as its resident lord. but now Dutton Halt is, like so many other memorials of a past social condition, a farmstead. Its present res- pected occupant, Mr. Thomas Baxter, C.C., and his family have, with reverent hands, loving care, and judi- cious taste, removed many of its incongruous utilitarian disfigurements, which for a lengthened period have hid- den its distinctive characteristics, and by skilful restor- ation have re-discovered and preserved a relic of the past which to antiquarian, historian, and those of lesser lore must always possess a unique and lasting interest - • " Liverpool Daily Post."

TEIE REV. G. FIA.RBOTTLE'S FAREWELL,

At B )ld-s:reet Weibyarl Cnap3l, Warriagson, on Suisday evening the Rev. W. Potts, the superin- tendent of the circuit, bade farewell of his congregatioa, and made feeliog reference to his stay in Warrington. On the following da; he depart 'd Llandudno where he will be stationed for tha next three years. Tile Rev. G. Hari) attle who is proceeding to Leicester also preached his farewell on Sand ay in Bews3y- road Wesleyan Caapel. He based his discourse on the text" Tiers be many that Ray, woo wel shew us any good ? Lord, lift thou up the light of Thy countenance upon us." Psalm IV. v. 6. He referred to the cause and cure oC pessimism, and at the close of his remarks said a 3 tha•; was the last service of his term of ministry amongst tnean it was fitting that he snoul.l mike a few remarks on tne suojact. There were many things in his mind that it would be impa3sible far him to sneak upon because they touched him too deeply. They would understood that it was with mingled feelings he reviewed one's life at the close of a three year's term. Things that one had d ane one fell; might, have been better done. Many a thing rose upon one's mitid as a possibility in quiet hours of thought but a kind of un- belief hindered one from attemp-ing therm Mach that , had been done one felt might have been better done, and in the light of possibilities of one's life there was : a natural regret in the review of a three years' term of service. With regard to the congregation itself no man could have had more sympathy, m ire loyalty and a more generous spirit extended to him. He could not recall as he stood there anything that had been said or done to him that the remembrance ef in after years would give him pain. There were special times that he should never forget when he was wonderfully sustained 'an l supported by the sympathy and help of the congre- gation. Be had always felt a great intereat in the young, and in the work connected with, the guild. He wished to express the hops that the younger portion of the congregation would feel that that place was a home to them in which they took a pride and pleasure, and would even be moved to offer a prayer that God's blessing might come upon it. This tri-ennial uprooting of a man ; this breaking of tender bonds was trying in many ways but as he had pointed out in the sermon en° answer that religion made to the question " Who will show.usjany good " was the answer given in the truth of immortality. Tnere was no better satisfac- tion in this life than came out of the love and affection of p.ople for one another, A love and affection whicn he had had a large share of in connection with that con- gregation. Triers was a great and blessed hope of meeting in another life where there would be no parting but where they would be ever with the Lord. The choir gave an excellent interpretation of tne anthem "Jesus Priceless Treasure " (Dr. Roberts) the solo parts being taken by Mrs. Stone and Mr. Baker.

INTERESTING PRESENTATIONS.

There was a pleaeaet gathering in the Band Room con- nected with Bewsey-road Wesleyan Chapel on Menday evening when a sock! gathering of members of the guild and friends was held, and interesting presenta- tions made. The chair was tam by the Rv. G.:Har- bottle, who expressed his delight at seeing such a large gathering present. He had been chinking that he in- herited a spirit of pessimism, but nevertheless he had never carried any pessimiem into hi i teaching of re- ligion, and though he had felt downheart el he had always teoueht lc was his business to speak as hopefully and enthusiastically as he could. A solo entitled "rho Tryscing Tree" was then sung by Mr. Baker ; after which Mess. FURNESS stepped forward to make a presentation ,0F al album containing the partraite of the members of the Ledie,' Class to Mrs. Harbottle. In doing so she said it had not been an easy matter for Mrs. Eiarbottle to lead a class such as theirs. But she had been more than a leader to them; she had been their friend. (Hear, hear.) They would all miss her and their minister also. Whilst regretting their removal, they hoped their IOS3 at Bewsev-road would be toe gain of friends at Leicester. In slieht recognition of Mrs. Harbottle's kindness the members asked her to accept 'the album with their pbetegraphs inside. They hoped that the present they had ceosen would meet with her aporeval. (applause.) Mrs. HARBOTTLE, who had a cordial receptionoaid she 'was very mach touched by the kind appreciation which had been shown for her servicls. That was the first iatimation she had had of the present, and she did not think they could have chosen anything which would have been more acceptable. She was very much obliged to thorn for it and she was sure Mrs. Furness had given her greater credit than she deserved. She had not done as much as she would have liked, but she greatly appre- ciated the kindness which had been shown to her, and she shoiald leave Warrington with a sorrowfu heart. Teat day was the anniversary of her wedding, she having been married 22 years. She hoped the recognition of her services that night would encourage , her to do more to help God's cause. Whenever she looked at that album it would bring very many precious seasons to her mind. The members of the class had shown a Christian spirit at all times. and she hoped that in eche future they would have a much better leader than she had been. Sh again thanked them and hoped that God would bless them and help them. (Applause). MISS GERTIE SHAW GREEN at this stage of the pro- ceedings gave a careful interpretation of a piece by Sir H. Parry on the violin. Mr. PARKINSON teen asked Mr. J. W. Havwarcl, J.P., to make a presentation of a writing cebin t to the. Rev. G: Harbottle, on behalf of the church. He remarked that the present had been subscribed by every member of the churcn. He looked upen it as a gift from the en- tire church. _(Applause).

• . Mr. J. W. HAYWARD th • c 'urge of his remarks re- ferred b Mr. Harbottle's ability as a preacher, and said that his sermons always bore evidence of careful thought and preparation. fie always told them sentething which was worth listening to. Then he (Mr. Hayward) appre- ciated him much for his gentlemanly qualities. Mr. Harbottle had always been a gentleman amongst them. He never passed a workingmen connected with them however ragged his clothes might be without acknow- ledging him. Mr. Harbottle had a heart to sympathise With the "horny-handed sons of toil." Be had ever been a welcome visitor wherever sorrow had been and had breueht comfor; aed ceneolatioe to the hears the afflicted and bereaved ones. As a worker in the Sunday school they bad not had such a helper in Sunday school work since the Rev Agar Beet. (Hear, hear.)Mr. Harbottle hal been to their Silver-street school as eften as it was posible and sometimes he must have come at some sacrifice to his comfort. They now wished him God speed and coneratiVated him upon the sphere of labour to which he had been called. Mr. Harbottle was going to Leicester and those who knew him would admit that he was the man for such a town. There they wanted men who would p t the Christian truth before them with logical reasoning and he did not know anyone better valified t do it thaa Mr. Harbettle.(Hear,hear.) There were six Wesleyan rnieistere at Leicesier and Mr. Harbottle would take the second place amongst them. Tney prayed that the God to whom he had yielded his heart and service might bless him abundantly and make him a blessing to others. He would now ask Mr. Bar- bottle to accept the present which was a spontaneous offering from members of the church. (Applause.) The Rev. G. HARBOTTLE had a hearty reception on rising to respond. He said his wife had already told them that it was their wedding day, and he regarded it as a happy omen for him and his wife that that meeting should have been held on that day. Future anniversaries of their wedding day would call uo at once the faces present at that meeting. He hoped the best side of life was still to come. A. :ban ought to be married once ;it taught him so much. (Laughter.) He had been mar- ried 22 years and yet that seemed a very brief period to

him, and the wedjing day seemed but as yesterday. After referring to Mr. Parkinson and Mr. Hayward who had spoken of his work, he said they must avat expect him to think that he deserved a tithe of what had been said. He had endeavoured in the pulpit and in his teaching, to give them ths best of which he was capable. They had taught him a good deal, and he believed he was a better preacher now than when he c ima If they thank.d him in their hes.rts as their presents seemed to indicat., it inspird him with hope and confidence for his work in Leicester. The writing cabinet which they had presented to him was suggastive of work and that was an important matter in a minister's life. It would be a source of joy and pride to him to show the people of Leicester that Bewsey-rnad regarded him worthy of such appreciation. That meeting would stimulate him, strengthen him,and help him in the week that lay before him, (Applause.) Mr. HORTON made a kindly ref 2.tsnce to the work of Mr. garb Ittle and recalled some of his remarks to them. He departed with their good wishes and goodwill and they trusted his wife and daughter would bt, very happy and very useful in their new sphere of work. ( Applause.)

• Councillor SHAW GREEN remarked that he was very proud to hive the opportunity of adding a word or two to what had been said. Like those who had preceded him he had thoroughly enjoyed, and he believed bene- fitted by Mr. Harbottle ministry. They were now part- jn him and giving him a happy send•off. He hoped the brightness of that gathering would permiate the whole of his life and that when he got to Leicester he would give them EMMA of those hri Want

nuggets which they had had from him at War- rington. That had been tho charm of Mr. Hat- bottle's teaching, it seem to him that when Mr. Harbottle forgot his manuscript there came out of him some of those rich thoughts which they could never for- get. At Leicester he would have a congregation who would be willing to hear him and if he had only round him a band of people like that present he would have reason to bless G:4 and take courage. (Applauss.) Councillor TINNION also expressed his appreciation of the work of Mr. Harbottle in the circuit. He hid en- joyed the serm ons of Mr. Harbottle equally or better than those of other ministers he had remembered he ar- ing. He had been a seatholder for 21 years, and during that time he had seen a good many changes, but there was no ministers who had done him more get.d and given him greater satisfaction than Mr. Harbottle had done. He considered that Mr. Harbottle had been a good pastor. He had been a man who bad visited his people,and that was a great failing with some minister He wished to express th 3 hope that bright as Mr. Hers_ bottle's time had been with them, it might be still brighter at Leicester; and that both he and his wife might be long spared to enjoy each others happiness and society. He also trusted that when Mr. Harbottle bad finishel his circuit term at Leicester they would look upon him and his work with the 'same pleasure and appreciation as they had dune in Warrington. (Ap- plause.) Light refreshments were then handed round. On re- suming the business of the meeting. Mr. CORBETT, in a few well chosen words, presented the Chairman with an exceptioeally fine,hand bag, as a token of the respect and esteem of the members of the Bewsey-road Guild. He referred to the fact that it was through Mr. Harbottle's efforts that the Guild was organised, and he had also contributed largely to its success. The speaker also referred to Mrs. Harbottle's services.

In acknowledging the gift, the Rev. G. HARBOT TIE said he held that every organized church ought to have its own guild of young men and young women. (Hear, hear.) No part of his work had given him greater pleasure than that in connection with the Guild at Bewsey-road. He would now like to put on the table an umbrella he had received from his class. The presents were symbolical of work,travel and trouble. The cabinet would represent the work ; the bag travel, and the umbrella symbolised defence. He should be un- worthy of his position if he did net go to his new circuit with a brave heart. (Applause.) Mrs. STONE said the choir had not forgotten the services of Miss Harbottle. She had been very regular in her attendance and a very active member. Tbey were sorry to lose her but if she came to Warrington again they would be glad to see her. Mrs Stone then presented Miss B:arbottle with a suitable present on behalf cf the choir. Her father acknowledged the gift on her behalf. Mrs Stone sang" The Last Milestone "and Mr Baker "The Friar of Oriers Gray," the accompaniments were played by Mr Wadsworth. A pleasant gathering con- chided with the sineing of the bymn " God be with you till we meet again."

WATER POLO CHAIIPIONSHIP

ST. HELENS v. WARRINGTON.

The return League match between St. Helens and Warrington, was played at the St. Helens Baths, on Tuesday eveuing,before a large attendance. The match was preceded by the return Combination game between the second teams of the above clubs. As the initial en- counter had resulted its a two goals to none victory for Warrington, St. Helens were naturally anxious to turn the tablAs, but failed. A very even game was witnessed, the final score being : Warrington 2 goals, St. Helens nil.

For the championship match, the St. Helens club placed their full team in the water, and Warrington were also well represented. At four minutes past nine the following players entered the water, under the command of Mr. C. Snowdon, of Liverpool. St. Helens.—J Aspinall goal; J Bretton and W Preston, full backs ; R Ripley, half back:; W Walker, J Mavity and J Lawrenson, forwards. Warrington.—A G Robertson, goal ' • Cronshaw and Boscow, full backs; W Ashton, halfback; J Hughes, C Ashton, and A Ashton, forwards. From the start the play was of a most exciting and interesting nature. Mavity going for all he was worth, defeated C Ashton for possession' and gave to Bretton, who handed over to Walker, and he in turn transferred to Lawrenson. Boscow, however, fouled him, and the ball was quickly sent to the other end, where Hughes shot outside. From the throw, Mavity banged the ball past Robertson amidst tremendous excitement after about half a minute's play. Again Mavity defeated Ashton in the swim off, and quickly gave to Ripley, who with one of his grand shots sent the leather with terrific force to Robertson, but the ball bounced over the crossbar. The game was keenly contested. Ripley, after a tricky swim. passed to Bretton, who returned the ball to his captain, and he swam down towards Robertson and transferred to Lawrenson, but the wing custodian proved too smart for Lawrenson's shot, and saved, although at the expense of a corner. At the othe - end Aspinall was called upon te cope with a fast shoz from A. Ashton, who managed to leave Mavity, and this be (Aspinall) did in really brilliant fashion, and threw down to Ripley. The home captain swam down, and when close up to the Warrington custodian sent in such a shot that Robertson failed to bold the ball and it went through. This further success against the con- querors of Everton (in a League match) fairly roused Moe spectators to a high pitch of excitement, and it was sometime before the game could be resumed Walker and Lawrenson further tested the War- rington goalkeeper, but without success. Just before the interval a corner was granted St. Helens, and this was taken by Walker, who threw across the goal to ' Lawrenson, and enabled him to put on a third. Ripley had hard lines with a splendid shot, and although the Warringtonians made a combined attack on the Saints' goal, nothing further was scored. Half-time: St. Helens, 3 goals; Warrington, nil. St. Helens now had to defend the deep goal. Ashton was again defeated for possession in the swi-n, bu ' Hughes managed to get in a swtft shot at A.spinall's charge, but Bretton cleared, and Walker once more lowered the ." Wirepullera' " colours. The excitement eventually toned down a bit, as ic was seen that the Saints had their doughty opponents well in hand. Robertson played a marvellous game, as also did Aspin- all, both custodians saving some very stiff shots. C. Ashton eventually sent the ball down to the Saints' goal, and enabled Arthur Ashton to put the finishing touch to it by sending it past Aspinall. Final • St. Helens, 4 goals; Warrington, 1 goal. The St. Helens Club have now a very heavy and im- portant list of engagements to fulfil. For the first time in the annals of the club the team has reaehed the semi-final stages of three important championships. On Thursday next they meet Everton at Liverpool in the Lancashire County contest ;on Tuesday next they en-, counter the famous Manchester 03birne team at Liver- pool in the Northern Counties Championship ; and early in September they meet Leicester in the semifinal of the English Championship. In addition to these water polo engagements, the St. Helene Club has also reached the final tie of the Grand Challenge Squadron Cempetition for the third year in succession.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY.

There was an average attendance at the meeting of this Society on Sunday afternom in Wycliffe Church. The organ was presided at by Mr. W. H. rayton with his accustomed ability. Toe service was also taken part in by Mr. S. Rowlands and Mr. Healey.

GOSPEL HALL.

On Sunday at this hall, Mr. F. Broadhurst preached from Psalm 40, 1-5, illustrating the subject by a descrip- tion of a visit to the lead mines at Snail-beach in Shropshire, which are 1,800 feet deep. To go down it, he said, was optional, so God had also left man a free will in regard to his eternal destiny. Mere the descent a change of clothcs was necessary, miners clothes hav- ing to be put on,and before a person could enter Heaven he centended there must be a change. a new birth. In descending, after the first few moments, the sensation was that you were going up.but entrance into darkness soon confirmed the fact that you were going down. Many people sincerely believed they were going un to Heaven, their way seemen rieht,but unless it was God's way, Jesus Christ, it will end in darkness and death. The bottom of the mines was reached by means of the cage, ladders and a shute, there being different stages of depth, so also there were different classes of sinners, some were on the first stage highly respectable, moral and religious, others had gone into deep depths of sin but both classes were in the pit and in darkness, and unless the power of God delivPrad them both would be lost for ever. God's power for lifting out of the pit was the Gospel.

COMMERCIAL FAILURES. —According t) "Kemp's Mer- cantile Gazette," the uumber of failures in England and Wales gazetted during the week ending August 19th, was 92. The number in the corresponding week of last year was 84, showing an increase of 8, being a net decrease in 1899, to data of 69.

THE WARRINGTON

friends and members of the Warrington Corps St.-Joht: Ambulance Brigade, in recognition of honorary services rendered to the eause. August 19th, 1899." Mr. HARDY, in reply, said he did not expect that a presentation would be made to him for what he bad done, although, of course, he was pleased if he had bees of any service to the brigade. Unfortunately he was out of the district now, and regretted his severeuca from the corps.

Captain WARING pointed out that Mr. Tay!or, the present bon secretary, should hays received a prize last year but he was unable. to receive it. He had been of great assistance to him, and he had pleasure in present- ing him, on his own behalf, with a preserve dish. (Ap- plause.) The Chief Superintendent added that he hsid been told by the Colonel that the corps was the best he had inspected that year. He was afraid there was a little bit of soft-soap about the remark—(laughter)—but It was only right that they should give him three hearty cheers.

The request was heartily complied with, cheers being also given for Mrs. Trimble. In teply,the COLONEL said he was exceedingly obliged to all present for their kind appreciation of his services, but their thanks were mostly due to Mr. and Miss Naylor for the use of their beautiful grounds in which to hole the inspcction, as well as for their hospitality. He made the remark privately to Captain Waring, but as he had in turn made it public he had no hesitation in stating that the inspection was the best he had had thi year. (kpplanse.) Miss Naylor presented the following prizes to the men for best attendances :—W D Fell, 36 marks, half-a. dozen knifes and forks ; T Langley, 34, cruet ; (these are second-class supernumerary officers) ; Private Kirby. 33, cruet; Private Lennon, 33, cruet •, Private Pittman, 33, cruet ; and Private W Newnes, 31 cruet. The donors of these prizes were Mr W B Roberts, Dr Markel and Captain Waring. Miss Naylor also kindly presented a number of certificates. At six o'clock an excellent tea was served in the Tea. perance Hall. At the conclusion Captain WatuNo said that that was the third time he had had to propose a vote of thanks to their kind host and bosteas for their great hospitality. (Applause.) They had dcna justice to the good things which had been provided, and it was only right that they should acknowledge their indebtedness to Mr. and Miss Naylor before separating. They had spent a very pleasant afternoon.

Three hearty cheers having bees gives, Miss Naimoli, who was received with hearty cheers, remarked that the applause which had just been accor- ded te her was much longer than the speech she inten- ded to deliver. They had been exceedin rly pleased to see the corps at the Hall, and she was much obliged to them for their kind vote of thanka. Her brother would no doubt respond on his own behelt. She again acknovis ledged the kind vote of thanks. (Applasee.) Mr. NAYLOR, who was also well received, responded, and remarked that, of course, it was very gratifying to know that one's work was appreciated as it appeared to be from the rapturous applause which had been given. It had been a source of great pleasure to Miss Naylor and himself to have had an opportunity of entertaining them ence mare in that building and outside it, and he was sure that the work carried on by Captain Waring and the corps,sometimes under very difficult conditions, deserved every encouragement from outsiders, but who were in full sympathy with the aims and objects of the St. John Ambulance Brigade. It was gratifying to know that nearly all over the country the work was being taken up with great spirit. On Thursday last the Lord Mayor of Manchester presented 131 certificates and prizes to the city police force who had passed the recognized exami- nation in connection with the association, and that day it must have been a great pleasure to those who had been enrolled as members of the 'Warrington Corps to have heard such an encouraging report from the bps of the Commissioner. He aunposed that what he realty meant to say was that during the present season he hah not met with a more efficient brigade than the one which had that afternoon gone threugh an inspection on the lawn, and that must be a gratification to Captain Waring and all the other officers, as well as to the rank and file. The work the Society aimed at was of a very exalted character. It was to relieve suffering humanity and many times the members who ha i received instruction had been able to render first aid in such ac efficient manner ,that limbs had been saved and. also lives. In these circumstances those who rejoiced in any effort made to relieve suffer- ing humanity in any form must be proud and delighted to find that in Warrington there was such an efficient detachment, and one so well worth their sympathy and support. It was a pleasure to be permitted to do some little :to show that he was in thorough har- mony with the work of the association, and it had been a great satisfaction to his sister and himself to have had the annual inspection at Cuerden Hall. Perhaps the corps wonld not have been invited but for the sugges- of the worthy Superintendent, who had the impression that there would be a mach greater muster if they were invited te the Hall, and Captain's Waring's impression had been fully realised. The band, a very efficient one, had shown their sympathy with the corps in a very practical way by discoursing sweet music for them without fee or reward. (Applause.) After some further observations, Mr. Naylor concluded by savirag that he hoped they would be able to meet together on some fut.ire occasion, and to both the institutions—the cricket club and the corps—he and his sister wished very great suacess. (Applause.) A tote of thanks having been accorded to the band, the visitors returned to the grounds, where the band played for dancing on the lawn for a short time. At shout half-past eight a start was made in the 'bases for Warringtcn. Several popular airs were played at the hall crates, and a happy gathering gave cheers for Mr and Miss Naylor as the homeward journey was com- meneed.

ALLEGED ASSAULT ON A MOTHER IN-LAW. At the Warrington Occasional Court, on Tuesday, before Mr. P. A. Fro3t, Peter Phillips, of Sankey, was charged with assaulting Jane Drummond on Saturday night.--Mr. Browne represented the com- plainant and said that she was mother-in-law to the defendant, and resided at 19, Foster-street, Widnes. The defendant lived at Bewsey Farm, Sankey. In consequence of the complainant hearing that the defendant was going to be prosecuted by the N,S.P.C.C., she decided to inquire into the matter, and on Saturday last she went to the defendant's house. When she arrived there she saw the defen- dant, and she asked him if her daughter was at home, and he replied that she was not. Convers- ation ensued, in which defendant said something disgusting about the complainant's late husband, ana when complainant denied the charge he struck her in the eye, and while she was on the ground bruised her terribly about the body and head. She managed to get into Lodge-lane, where sbe fainted, being subsequently takea to the Warrington In- firmary where she was medically attended. her son, a licensed victualler, obtained a I ree warrant against the defendant on Monday morning. Mr. Browne applied for an adjournment of the case to the Warrington County Petty Sessions to be held on September 6th.—Complainant corroborated Mr ; Browne's statement,and ultimately the me was re- mended as desired.
