AMBULANCE CORPS AT CUERDEN HALL.

1 With their usual generosity Mr. R. A. Naylor and Miss Naylor renewed their invitation to the mem- bers of the Warrington Corps of the St John Abulance Brigade to pay their annual visit to Cuerden Hall, Thelwall, and this event took place on Saturday in beautiful weather. Mr. Naylor's quiet retreat is more familiarly known as Oyster Shell Hall. It is prettily situated an.' is sheltered from the roadway by surrounding elms and beeches. Nothing seems to disturb the stillness save the singing of the birds which are piping here and everywhere. The grounds cannot be described as extensive but they are well laid out and trimly kept. In one shady nook several pleasant-looking summer houses are studded with oyster shells, aid these have probably had something to do with the name which the hall bore at one time. The hall has been considerably improved and enlarged by Mr. Naylor with artistic taste: Entering the recep- tion hall from a porch which runs underneath an extensive verandah, the visitor is introduced to the host and hostess who stand at the top of a beauti- fully polished staircase. Many availed themselves of the privilege of inspecting the rooms, which are exquisitely furnished. The mansion is rich in pictures, old china, antique cabinets, treasured nick-nacks and articles of vertu innumerable. Mr. Naylor is especially a great connoisseur of china,and every room bears evidence of it. The entrance hall is unequalled in the district, and contains some fine pieces of statuary, coats of mail, ex- pensive skins, and other articles of great value and in- terest. The prettiest room is a combination drawing- , room with its richly carved oak furniture, its luxurious carpets, curtains and embroidery, and choice old china- ware. One of the latest adlitions to the curios is a very unique sack earthenware bottle which was recently sent to Mr. Naylor from a South Staffordshire village. The bottle which is wound round with a thin wire, owing to its great age, bears the following inscription :—" Hugt, 0 Toft is Bottle 20th May, 1600." Having made an in- epection of the rooms, the visitor was free to wander about the grounds, and indulge in games on the lawn. Cricket was also engaged in, in one of the fields. Mr and MiSg Naylor did their utmosz to make the visit an enjoy- able one. Miss Naylor was charmingly attired in white and at the close of the inspection of the ambulance corps she gracefully presented the prizes and certificates. Dancing was indulged in on the lawn to the strains of the Borough Baud, under the cenductorship ot Mr. Griffin, and it may be here stated that the bandsmen gave their services gratis. Amongst those present on the lawn to witneso the in- spection, in addition to Mr. and Miss Naylor, were the Deputy-Commissioner, the Hon. Lieutenaet Colonel C J Trimble, M.D. (Preston), who cenducted the inspection; Mr George R Crosfield (hon treasurer), Captain Waring (chief superintendent). Mr. Buckley (superintendent of stores), Mr. F raylor (acting lion secretary), Dr. Parker (Stockton Heath), Dr. Dyer (of Cheltenham, on a visit to Mr Naylor); Mr and Mrs Bylanda, Me and Mrs Edwin Woods, Mrs Waring, Mr William Houghton, Miss Jack- son. Mr and Mrs T Mitchell, and Mr T Birtles, the last named after the inspection photograph- ing the members of the brigade, the visitors and the band. Mr R Pierpoint, M.P. (president), was prevented attending owing to the death of a friend whose funeral he bad to attend, and Chief Surgeon Adams, Hon. Surgeon Reginald Smith, Dr. Bowers, Dr. Leadbetter, Sir Gilbert Greenall and Mr J C Parr were also unable to be present for various reasons. The visitors from Warrington, who numbered abeut 250, were, by the klndnes of Mr. Naylor, conveyed to the Hall in several large 'buses supplied by Mr. Edward Connor, and these left the Central and Bank Quay Rail- way Stations at th.ee o'clock. The visitors, who thoroughly enjoyed themselves, were acccinpanied by the Warrington Workingmen's Mission Cricket Club. They were all entertained to tea in the Temperance Mission Hall. The arrangements were in the hands of " Captain Waring, and they were entirely satisfactory. At the outset the brigade went through the usual exercises, under the direction of Superintennent Buck- " ley. This was followed by stretcher drill, which was " admirably done, notwithstanding a little apparent ner- vousness at the commencement. Forty men and five ' officers wore uniform, many of them for the first time, and there were about 50 men who are not yet supplied with it, in plain clothes. The men, who are connected with the various works, railway companies of Warring- ton, and the Ship Canal, looked very smart and enthu- siastic in their work• At the conclusion of the inspec- tion the men were drawn up in line. Celonel Tniernex addressed them and said he was perfectly satiefied with everything he had seen that day, and particularly with the work of the men in uniform. He knew from what the Chief Suaerintenderat had told him that some of the men had just been put into uni- form for the first time, and their work was particuberly smart. The public could not expect ambulance men to show the preciston and smartness of Volunteers, or the exactness of trained soldiers, but it was expected from them that they should exhibit some degree of smartness; in fact it was absolutely necessary that they should do so, as they could not go from place to place with their stretchers and other appliances unless they moved to some extent as the military did. The importance of drill was particularly finpressed upon his mind on Jubilee Day in London, as it was there coca that if the ambulance men had not possessed some military knowledge it would have been absolutely impossible for them to have taken up their various positions. As he had already said, the drill that day had been particu- larly good for ambulance men, and the Superintendent seemed to have a good knowledge of the work, being able to handle the members with satisfaction to him (the inspector). Ihe Colonel then proceeded to point out one or two minor defects, asking the men to avoid balking in the ranks, although he admitted that it •aas difficult to resist the temptation to point out a defect when one man saw anther making a mistake. Silence should be observed as far as possible. The corps seemed to be making satisfactory progress in Warrington and district, and as deputy-commissioner in charge of the district it would afford him very reat pleasure to con- vey to the Commissioner the excellent appearance of the men and the good character of the work which had been done. He congratulated them on the strength of the corps, while to those in multi he wished to say he had not ask much ambulancce work from them, as some of them were old members and were able to do well ; and also because he did not wish to interfere with the rest of the programme. (Hoer, hear, and applause.) , Captain WARING then stepped forward and asked Mies Naylor to make a presentatton to Mr. Hardy, who wee formerly connected with the corps, to which he has rendered valuable services as lion secretary. He had, I however, had to leave Warrineton to undertake more " responsible duties at Frodingham, on the Great Central Railway.

MIPS NAYLOR expressed the pleasure it afforded her to make the presentitlon and then handed to Mr. Hardy a handsome electro-plate tea and coffee pot, sugar basin, and cream jug. One of the articles contained the following inscription :—" Presented to T. H. Hardy by

THE EXAMINER.

AUGUST 25th 1899

AN impression prevails Are Transvaal AffAirs that the relations be- lonproving ? tween this country and the Transvaal are improving. We hope that this is a correct account of the situation, though it hardly accords with the general belief. The tor- tuous character of Pre:ident KRUGER'S policy is still maintained. He prefers not to give a Wain answer to a plain question. The report is that he has actually sent no answer to the pro- posal for an inquiry, after delays and negotiations 'with Bloemfontein and Capetown extending over several weeks. We believe this account is most likely correct, because it accords with the policy President KRUGER has pursued before. For a plain, simple-minded man, he loves tortuous tac- tics with an affection which casts doubt upon his sincerity altogether. He dodges, and this is net a feature of diplomacy which disposes persons t o have much confidence in a diplomatist who prac- tices the method. What he is understood to now propose is a term of franchise with conditions exactly the same as he resisted at Bloemfontein last month. What has made him change his position? The desire not to allow the COLONIAL SECRETARY to score a single point in the contest, the pressure of opinion at the Cape, and the obvious hardening of public feeling in this country, along with some degree of preparation for a rupture of the diplomatic friendliness which has been subjected to so many severe strains. If we distrust the new move it is because President KRUGER has been educating us in his policy of proffering plausible schemes and then beginning to evade them the moment they are accepted. This Want of frankness does not please his apologists in this country, or persons like ourselves, who desire to hold the balance even between national and party interests. It makes us suspicious. The franchise question is only one of several questions, but if the,Outlanders once gain a genuine franchise, with a real representation, they can do very much to help themselves, and to cope with some of the minor tyrannies under which they suffer. Our general aim, as a country, is to allow the Trans- vaal to retain its quasi-independence in foreign affairs, and its complete domestic independence when all the promises made to our countrymen of perfect equality have been fulfilled. It is not clear that the Boer really wants to live on peace- able terms with us. The more ardent believe they can inflict upon us another military disaster, and that then we shall be glad to leave them alone al- together. What President KRUGER and General OUBERT think of the results of a war, we do not know. They are wisely silent. When they speak at all, it is to say that, if independence cannot be ,kept by diplomacy, it may as well be lost by war. That is, if the Outlanders are to have the same power as the Boers, the Boers will soon be out- numbered and outvoted. They may be so, despite anything the Free State can do, and the most treacherous Boers in Natal can do. The choice may really lie between painless extinction and a N,,...yiolent death.

WE should be sorry to think Paris and the Republic. that Sunday's rioting in Paris was a grave symptom. As there 'were cries of " Vive le R6publique " the riotet s Were not consciously demonstrating for the Bonapartists or the Orleanists, which is so far satisfactory. The unruly elements in Paris are always a considerable force, and they have to be dealt with severely, and often, as it seems to Englishmen, even cruelly. The details of the disturbances, however, are less important than th

motives which animated the bands of young men Who have taken to street-rioting to give expression to their views and feelings. The police and the soldiers, if firmly directed, are quite able to pre- vent any grave unsettlement. The Government Will be strongly supported in any measures it •thay deem necessary for the preservation of order; and until the Rmnes trial comes to an

end there ought to be no chance for the noisy and the unruly. Behind much of the existing disquiet are the enemies of the Republic, and they alone can profit by the dis-

order. It would almost appear, however, that the chance of overthrowing the Republic has gone by, and when the Chambers meet the Government may be defeated instead. The Bonapartists are hardly made of the stern stuff which achieves revolutions. The leaders do not care to openly appear, and the Princes are afraid of their skins. The rank and file of the Bonapartist faction are not formidable, and nothing sorious could be achieved without the aid of the army. The army is not political, as it used to be, and it has to think of France rather than of the families which would

like to govern it once more. What . could Bona-

Oartism- do at the present moment for Fi ance P libthing, absolutely nothing. It is a moribund idea. The recovery of the lost provinces has not Egured in any of the speeches made by the exiles. To promulgate the intention of taking active steps to advance to the Rhine would soon bring France to the brink of war, and Germany would have something to say in the matter at once, and ii a way to command respect. Probably there

Would be a declantion of war before a Bonapartist Government could be well installed. The Orlean- ists are rich, and not very capable agitators. There je net much danger to be apprehended from them. The Republic,therefore, would seem to be safe for 'the present. The generals who have been swear- irig at Rennes could hardly be trusted to l'prrn a Government. Their credulity is as re-

Markable as their intellectual incapacity. Besides, "what could they promise, to: win over the army?

===== 0002601_18990825_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
The army has not been assailed by anybody, though the General Staff is coming very badly out of the inquiry. The evidence that Jewish officers are selling military papers to the enemy is a mere freak of a disordered imagination. The caseselected for proof is breaking down every day. We may hope, then, that neither tumults in Paris nor attempts to excite the army will have any effect in endangering the stability of the Republic. There is no other form of government that has any substantial and popular promises to make, and there is some reason for beLeving that France will once more rise superior to the effervescence of the moment.

It is very well known that certain pottery occu- pations are injurious to the sight, but it remained for Mr. Snell, at the meeting of the British Medi- cal Association, to draw attention to the grinders employed at iron and steel works. It seems to be a frequent thing for particles to lodge in the corner, and when the men wear glasses they become studded by the farce of the impact. The obvious moral of Mr. Snell's paper which has just been published, is to use glasses, and there is an end of the matter. One fact about the eye, how- ever, concerning which the public are generally under a wrong impression, is the effect of the use of magnifying glasses by watchmakers. That well-known oculist, Mr. Brudenell Carter, stated some years ago it was quite the exception to have a watchmaker come to him for treatment. The premature use of glasses is injurious, and that is why people who think they want spectacles should be careful to have their eyes tested by thoroughly competent persons ; but the occasional use of magnifying glass, with the corrective of practice of the ordin- ary vision appears to do no harm. The eye is like a machine in the sense that it is liable to wear, and the use of small print without the means of enlarging it is likely, to use up the sight; and small print should be avoided. Even here, however, the nervous condition of the person appears to be an element. One of the millionaire newspaper pro- prietors in New York is a victim in this way to a partial loss of sight; but probably the anxiety he felt about his enterprise had something to do with this.

The trading capacity of women does not come out well in the bankruptcy returns for last year. There were 433 failures, of which 175 were cases of married women, 174 widows, and 84 spinters— an excess of 11 over the previous year. The lia- bilities were £248,420, and the assets £93,271. A table is given of the various occupations carried on by the 397 women who were in businesi. The trades most favoured stand in the following order: Grocery trade, 70 failures ; milliners, 46; drapers, 39; boot andshoomakers, 26 ; publicans, 18; lodg- in7-house keepers, 17; confectioners, 15; bakers, 13'; other occupations not reaching double figures. Taking out the persons of no occupation, we find that women in trade owed £165,725, and showed £71,615 assets. The individual amounts, as the Inspector remarks, are relatively insignificant. The probability is that, in the malerity of cases, the women were trading upon insufficient capital, opened shops in unsuitable neighbourhoods, were mistaken as to the businesses they acquired, and hoped for some charitable or friendly custom which they did not get. Statistics of former years agree with last year's figures in shevving that grocery, millinery, and drapery businesses attract women most, and hence the failures are greatest in these trades. Married women engaged in trade are exempt from the bankruptcy laws, and generally execute deeds of arrangement. The immunity gives rise to some abuses, to which the Inspector again calls attention. For instance, marriage during early proceedings stops bankruptcy.

The Warrington corps of the St. John Ambu- lance Brigade is in a flourishing condition, if we may judge by the excellent turn-out of last Satur- day on the occasion of the annual inspection, and when the Inspecting Officer expresses the opinion that it is the best corps he has inspected this year, it may be taken for granted that it is in a thoroughly efficient state. The movement is one that cannot fail to commend itself to anyone who has sympathy for suffering humanity. The mem- bers are drawn from the various works and rail- ways, and the course of training and instruction they receive enables them to render efficient aid in case of accident, and to promptly relieve the sufferings of the injured person. Much unneces- sary suffering has often been caused through a lack of knowledge as to the best course to adopt until medical assistance can lee obtained. This lamentable state of affairs is, happily, now being gradually removed, and with the continued p,pro- gress of the St. John Ambulance Brigade the ren- dering of first aid to the injured will not be marked by the blundering which has characterised many cases in the past. By inviting the corps to his hall, Mr. R. A. Naylor has not only shown his in- terest in the movement, but helped to popularise a commendable institution.

Throughout the country Wesleyan ministers are on the move to new circuits. This triennial re- moval has again raised the question of the itiner- ary system which John Wesley made such a dis- tinct feature of the Christian denomination which bears his name. The c)pinion seems to be steadily gaining ground that the three years' system is far too short. A Wesleyan minister may be said to have just got well in harness when the fiat of the Wesleyan Conference removes him to another sphere of work. The Rev. George Har- bottle, who has just been removed from Warrington to Leicester, described the system as a triennial uprooting of a man and stated;what must be abundantly clear to everyone, that the breaking of tender bonds which have been formed in a.circuit during a three years' residence, is try- ingin many ways. The question of extending the period has been repeatedly under discussion but no change has yet teen decided upon. A five years' term seems to possess many advantages which should commend it for general acceptance and with a steadily-growing opinion in favour of a change this may yet be achieved without in any way removinz. any of the distinctive features of Wesleyan Methodism.

Some interesting statistics have recently been published by the Labour Department of the Board of Trade with reference to the profit-sharing systein in the United Kingdom and the Colonies. Our readers are doubtless aware that this system has been adopted with conspicuous success by Messrs. Richmond and Company, Limited, gas apparatus manufacturers, of Academy-street. They are amongst the largest of the 85 firms now known to practise profit-sharing. The number mployed by these firms varies according to sea- sons, but the minimum is put down at 50,647, and the maximum at 53,010. Despite the fact that the number of firms has decreased by six as compared with the previous year, the number of employees shows a slight increase. A mean bonus of 5.5 per cent. in wages was paid in 75 cases, compared with 7.2 per cent. in 1897. Excluding 17 cases in which no bonus was earned, the bonus allotted in the re mining 58 cases was (taking into account the- number of participants in each case) at the mean rate of 11.8 per cent., compared with 11.4 per cent. in 1897. It cannot be said that profit shar- ing has been extensively taken up, but the good work which is now being done by the pioneers of this system cannot fail to have an effect upon others in the near future. At present trade is good throughout the country and firms are too busy to Inaugurate such a system. But it is certain that a system which tends to draw employers and employed nearer together and to make their interest in the welfare of the con- cern identical gratine4 fail to be an advantage, and one that must eventually receive a more general acceptance.

Now that the Warrington _Town Council is in possession of the Town Clerk's report on the Local Government Act of 1888, in which the position of county boroughs is clearly set forth, we may ex- pect to hear of some action being taken in the matter. The subject has already been under dis- cussion by the General Purposes Committee,and we understand that the.opinion prevailed that some

===== 0002601_18990825_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY AUGUST 25, 1899

steps should be taken to make Warrington a county borough, and once for all be free from the Lancashire County Council. It is a matter for re- gret that Warrington was not included amongst the County Boroughs when the Act CAME) into force. There was then a population of over 47,000 but to-day it is nearly 65,000 and yet the Corporation has less control over some matters of local importance than towns of far less population or rateable value. The advantages to be gained by the change to a county borough are patent to all who have token notice of the County Council's treatment of Warrington on such questions as the Technical Instruction grant and the widening of Warrington Bridge.

, Before taking such a decisivh step as seeking to separate ourselves from the county authority it is advisable that we should count the cost. There is little doubt that the Lancashire County Coun- cil will oppose the separation both at the local inquiry and in Parliament and the claim set up for compensation will be considerable. The War- rington Corporation is fully occupied at the present moment in developing a number of schemes such as waterworks' extensions and electrical supply besides having in hand the erection of new police buildings, a techniical institute, hospital ex- tensions and ,othar important matters. It can hardly be considered advisable to enter into such an expensive venture as that of making Warring- ton a County Borough before we have somewhat lightened the present burden. The most regret- table feature in connection with any delay which may take place is that the claim of the Lancashire County Council in the event of a separation will increased by the continued progress of the town.

RUNCORN NOTES. [By A CORRESPONDENT.] The need of public baths for Hano)rn has been illustrated this week in a two-fold manner. First by the sad death of o little boy anxious to have a bathe; and secondly by the swimming galmt at the Big Pool, when the inability of rtuncorn swimmers to secure prizes was so strikingly illustrated. Other illustrations, could be quoted galore.

Tthe air is full of comments upon the" Park and Common," and comparisons are being made be- tween the rejection of a scheme to convert, the Market Lail into a public bath at a cos of £2,300,- and the desire to spend £4,000 on the" Park and Common "—the one being rejected on a plea of "bread versus baths," and the other said to be sub- servient to the same argument. I think the public should not lose sight of the nature of its wants.

Baths are required, and the demand for them is becoming more and more strongly expressed by the ratepayers; and, though the park may be looked upon as a kind of luxury, yet it is one the public in their heart of hearts greatly desire At present they pay high rates and get very little f-r their money. Farther, it would appear as if the only econosnv possible in the administration of Uun- corn's affairs, is that of avoiding plain duty simply because it costs money. How did the rate get o high? Ah ! thereby hangs a story ; the telliKg of which would prove highly interesting.

But to return to the baths and the park. The public want—(demand)—the baths and mean to have them. The " park " they desire to have, and will try to get. Clearly then it is the duty of the "city fathers" to see that they get them—or as much of them as possible—quickly and cheaply. The ratepayers whilst paying so high a rate for "nothing "—will surely not object to paying 31 or 44 more for" something." Beside 3 a little economy, where there's room for it, will reduce tho-e men- strous figures—.E2,3oo for bathe,and .E 4,000 for park —to something like .£3,500 for both.

The Brewster Sessions will beheld at Runcorn on Monday next, and evidences of further attempts to secure an addition to the number of licensed pre- mises are again prevalent. Runcorn, however, is already greatly overstocked with public-houses, and it is to be hoped that those people so anxious to become engaged in the liquor traffic—naturally only because they want to accommodate a large number of their neighbours—may be frustrated. The granting of a license is a matter ef vital im- portance, and this we know the magistrates fully realise. The town does not require any more licensed premises and it is time that fact was realised.

Next week we will be crossing the border-line and entering into the football arena and conse- quently it is only likely we should speculate upon the merits and demerits of our own particular favourites. Personally I should like to say many things about the Runcorn tam, and the Runcorn football club in general but I refrain—this much, however, I will say the chances of a Successful reason are great. The team seems ready to tlke the field without any ruptures of the natilre of those so disastrous to the " club's affairs "last year, and a good start always augurs well.

During the excessively hot weather at present prevailing it may be well to warn people against the prevalence of cholera. Most people are tempted to overindulgence in fruit because of its luscious and tempting appearance. They should, however, refrain, and await the autumn of the cooler days. A few deaths have occurred already in our midst from cholera of some form or another—English or °she? modifications being applied to describe them. But the. fact remains we have to face a danger. Therefore, t behoves us to see to our sanitary arrangements more closely,and insist upon the flushing of our public sewers oftener.

===== 0002601_18990825_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
PROSPECTS OF FOOTBALL IN RUNCOTtN.

[BY TUE ZAMBESI. SCORCHBB.]

The prospe-cts of Football in Runcorn' are bright —all last year's men seem exceptionally fit, and during the practice matches are going as well as can be expected. Houghton, who has been enjoying a holiday at the Isle of Man, in every way appears fit. J. Butterworth is as tricky as ever, but would doiwell if he gave young Jones, of Widnes, a few more opportunities than he did last year. Jones is I a fast man and if anything, shows a slight improve- ment on last year's form as regards speed. Tommy Warder and Wilkinson and the younger Butter- worth can all be relied upon far sterling work when called upon. This gives us five reliable three-quarter men and with average luck this should carry us through the season. The half-back line it is sr.fficient to say will be occupied by the redoubt- able Jolley and Faulkner, backed up by Nickson as reserve, and there are also a few other promising men to fill up their positions if occasion requires. The pack is of course the most essential part of a Rugby team and it is upon their energy and stamina that the whole team depend. Well I think that Runcorn may rely upon their "eight," for out of the 14 men on the field on Tues- day night, it will not be difficult to select " eight " worthy of the traditions of Runcorn's palmiest days. Farmer, Walker, Darlington, Lightfoot, Gay- ter, Alf Taylor, Reece, Owen Hughes would make as lively a pack as any and the crowd that got over them would have to be going it strong. Runcorn's chances for a first place in the league should be rosy, and will be rosy from what I can see, as each one of the team and the reserve fully intend to make a bid for the upholding of the honour ot the good old town. The "red-tails "know how to play, if they only will, and when they ara trying, even such teams as Oldham have to go to the wall— " Patriotism can beat money "'any time—and so easy. What are the chances for the cup? About the same as those of last year if Runcorn are going to persist in holding their opponents too cheaply; but if they intend trying throughout---and I realyt believe they do—then I will predict that the club's new president will be the proudest man in the Northern Counties when he raises the cup on high, filled with the sparkling nectar. My advice to Runcorn is go in and win! You have the metal, the spirit, the enthusiasm, and a good reputation on your side; and are opposed by "money "—that is all. Therefere don't let the "filthy lucre "triumph over you this season. Remember a " United Run- corn can face the iootball world, and make it tremble too."

RUNCORN COTTAGE HOSPITAL FOND. SWIMMING GALA. AT RUNCORN.

I' rhe Runcorn Cottage Hospital Fund was founded some five years ago, through the energy of the members of the Runcorn Cycle Club, and several inflaential townsmen; its first secretaries being Messrs. W. E. Dudley and W. Mack. The object of the fund was to provide some accommodation for accidents, Runcorn being so full of industries of a peculiar and hazardous nature—where workingmen who received injury whilst following their employ- ment could be immediately attended to, without necessitating their removal by !train to Liverpool. At the commencement, cycle parades were the order of the day, but owing to Runcorn's everlasting desire for changes, some other method had to he devieed,and thus the swimming gala made its debut under Professor Finney. During its progress the fund has been accumulating, and now about £4OO lies ready for disposal. The advent of a generous philanthropist, who would provide a " cottage " suitable for the purpose, would enable the Com- mittee to apply their fund to maintenance; and that philanthropist would have the thanks of many a Runcorn workingman who through misadventure would partake of its benefits, and would at the same time give an impetus to the fund. Ileder the auspices of the Runcorn Cycle Club, a highly successful swimming gala took place at the Big Pool, Runcorn, on Monday last, the object being to further augment the Cottage Hospital Fund. There was a large and fashionable attendance, and considerable interest was evinced in the several items, which resulted as follows

GALA. AT THE BATtIS,

POLO MATCH.-NORI'll v. MIDLANDS.

A crowded attendance assembled at the Corporation Batts on Saturday evening ta witness a swimming gala organised by the Warringtm Swimming Club, the chief feature of which was a polo match between the repre- sentative of the North and Mtdland pOo teams. The other items of the programme were of an attractive character and the arrangements for the evening were carried cut most satisfactorily. The hon officials were : Judges, Messrs J Derbyshire, J Critchley and T Locker; handicapper and starter, Mr J Derbyshire; check starter, Mr G Graticds ; competitors' stewards, Messrs R Harding and C H Wood; bon secretaries, F Garside and A G Robertson. Councillor Dr. Canoe!l presided, and during an interval in the entertainment spoke of the advantages of a knowledge of swimming, and alluded briefly to the rapid strides made by the War- rington Swimming Club.

The first item was a hundred yards open handicap,for which there were 43 entries. There were no very "clasAy" eempetitors, but the heats were well competed and provided plenty of excitement. In the eight heat J E Cronshaw. Warrington, was disqualified for ;tieing before time. G Nevison, Wigan, and H Roberts, tiouth- port, swum a dead heat. Tiv tenth was alas a dead heat between W R Smith, Crosby, and T W Davies, Warrington. G B Green, Sailer d, a very young swimmer, bad a very ptpular victory in the ninth heat. The final was a most exciting race. L R Williams, Northwich,looked a likely winner until fifteen ynrd3 from home, when W A Sherlock, Salford, swum very strongly and won by a foot, half a yard separa tin! the second and third, Williams and May,Salford. An exhibition of trick swimming was given ley "Little Tich," whose age is eleven years, and who has won over 90 prizes. This was not his first appearance at the baths, and, as on .a previous occasion, his per- formance was received with every sign of pleaaure. T. Wright, of Oeborne S.C., gave interesting exhibitions of blindfold and high diving. He dived three times, ogi the Last occasion blindfolded, from a platform near the roof of the building, and afterwards dived from the height of the balcony blindfolded, and with arms and legs tied. His feats were watched with considerable in- terest, and their successful performance evoked hearty applause. J H Derbyshire (holder 100 yards world re- c•nel) gave a display of fast swimming against P Lister 1,008 yards champion, 1898. The international trial polo match was, of course, the principal item of the evenieg, and aroused great interest. TIZO Northern players have won five out of the six previous contests and lost one. For Saturday's match a stronger team could not have been chosen to represent the North, but one of the selected, W. H. Lis- ter, had obtained permission to be absent. This doubt- less weakened their back division somewhat, as Lister is at present playing the best back game in the cenetry, besides which his ability as a goal scorer is not to be dis- rgarded. Combined play between Derbyshire and Sharrocks led to the old champion adding a sixth goal, and the into?- vat was reached with the score six goals to nil. These rapid successes of the North tam created the greatest enthusi- asm, and evoked loud cheering. BEFORE the MAGISTRATES.

AN UNGRATEFUL WORKHOUSE INMATE. On Monday at the Borough Police Court, before the Mayor and other magistrates, a young woman named Sarah Jane Mannion, a widow, was charged with neg- lecting tier two children, who had in consequence be- come chargeable to the Unioo.—Mr. Sh anr prosecuted on behalf of the Guardians, and stated that the defen- dant and three childcen were admitted to the Workhouse about a year ago. She remained there until the begin- ning of the month, ani, as she had conducted herself fairly well, and seemed anxious to go out to earn her living, a ettuation was found for her as well as some clothing by the Ladies' Visiting Committee. A good out- fit was provided, and her wages were 53 a week. lie knew the house where she was engaged. It was a very comfortable home, but she only remained there a week, and had not since been near the Workhouse. The Guar- dians were naturally very indignant at her conduct, and asked him to prosecute her. She had treated all in connectirn with the house very badly.— Mrs. Ward, the matron, stated that shc remembered the defendant coming to the house in August, 1893. During the last few months cf her stay in the house she had been very good and had worked well, although at one time she was placed in class D owing to her misconduct. An excellent outfit and a very good home were found for her at a well-known farm house.—Mrs. Fernside, por- tress, said the defendant once worked for her and she did very well.—Defendant said she was unable to kneel as she was suffering from " housemaid's knee." She had not sufficient liberty allowed her at the farm.—Mr. Shearer said they had a large number of similar eases, and the Guardians had been put to great expense. None of the women stayed in situations for more than a week. —The Mayor said the ease would be adjourned for a month, and if in the meantime the defendant did not show an improvement, and a desire to maintain her children, ski would be severely dealt with. The Guar- dians and all concerns I appear to have been very kind to her.—Defendant : I shall give Mr. Shearer some- thing on account on Saturday. I have not deserted my children, because I am fond of them.

Everybody should use Lase high class soaps,.—Advt. MESSRS. RYLA.NDS BROS.' ENGINEERS' PICNIC.

The engineers cennected with Messrs. Itylands Bro. held their annual picnic on Saturday. An arrangement was entered into whereby the party joined Messrs. Thomas Cook and Sons' excursion to Chatsworth. A start was made shortly before nine o'clock, and Bakewell was reached at ten o'clock. Conveyances were in waiting, and the trippers did justice to an excellent luncheon. Subsequently a visit was made to Chatsworth, and needless to add the many interesting features at this historic mansion, together with the inspection of the beautiful grounds, formed not the least interesting item in the day's pleasure. The drive waA continued to Haddon Hall, and the ancestral seat of the Duke ot Portland proved especially interesting to the visitors. Continuing their drive, tha party next went to Matlock, where dinner was served at five , o'clock. The party now dispersed, many indulging in boating. A mishap occurred to one of the visitors who, having lost his hat, tried to recover it, hut he overbalanced himself and fell headlong into the water. Fortunately he was an adept. at the natatory art, and a few strokes landed him in safety appar- ently little the worse for his immersion. After dinner toast, including the " Town and Trade of Warrington," were honoured and short speeches delivered. The return jonrney was safely accomp- lished at eleven o'clock, praise being due to Messrs Jeffrey (secretary), Savory (treasurer), Berry,Peseol and J Gibson for their satigfactery arrangements. THE AFITRMATH, Bcc., Bcc.

BY MR. H. WIirTHAM, I would like to give readers of the " Examiner" some little explanation of the very peculiar position in which the United States Government finds it- self at the present time in its relationship with its I new possessions. The heading to my last communi- I cation Was " After the war," yet the war is not by I any means over. True, the war with Spain is over, ! ! the teams of peace have been agreed upon and ! ratified, and the sum of £4,000,000 has been paid to !Spain for all her rigersekee,&ce a the Philipines,and ! yet what Spain sold the American Government she has been unable to deliver. How is it? It is impos- sible to answer off-hand, how it happens that America entering into a war, its the name of free- dom, and all that is just, findes herself regarded as an oppressor by the very people whom she would ! liberate. ! Englishmen will understand the ensiti3a better ! if they will take as a parallel case the relationship I between the Roman Catholic population of Ireland and the British Gwernment. To me it seems that the greatest trouble, almost the only trouble the British government has had with Ireland has been with the Roman Catholic portion of its population. The impossibility of impressing upon them the fact that their freedom could only be an outcome of tht ir own actions, and honest acceptance of the re- sponsibiltties•of life. Does not this view of the question appeal still more to the Englishman when he looks around at his own peasantry? Go into the purely agricultural districts of Englani and study the peasant as an outcome of centuries of Established Church guardianship, and say what is the difference between hon and the Irish Catholicpeasant and the poor Philipines ? In each case the honest inquirer must confess with sorrow that he only sees "A thing that grieve. not and tint never hopes. Stolid an I stunned, a brother to the ox." For centuries the Roman Catholic Church has had the whole of the islands of the Paeific in her charge, and, with the aid of the Spanish Government has undoubtedly withheld from the peoples of those islands all the good influences this end of the century has to give. As a result of this selfish neglect, the United States Government has to deal with a people who are mentally unable t 3 understand the state of things. They cannot see why the American soldier having driven out the Spanish soldier, they are not left to govern themselves, and do pretty much as they like with what they consider their own. To add to their troubles, the Protestant missionary turns up, and they are made to believe that they are not only go- ing to be kept out of their political and civil rights but also their religion is to be taken from them. Of course the Catholic priest is playing a great part in all this, and is no small factor in prolonging the war. What everyone hepes for, and is looking for is that something will happen to Aquinaldo to take him off the scene. It is thought that should the rebels lose their leader, the rebellion would come to an end. Now, how is all this affecting the American mind? It is difficult to answer thie. It is quite possible that your English press is giving you more trust- worthy information than we in the States can get hold of. Not the least of our troubles is the cen- sorship that is carried out rather rigorously, yet possibly not more than is necessary. The American press is not a thing of beauty, and the joy it brings is very fleeting. How can the American press de- termine what is shall, or ought to do, when every day are printed fake interviews with responsible statesmen, only to be contradicted the day after. To day the Admiral of the fleet is made to utter. stuff that would stamp him a fool were it true Great head lines are used and there is a rush for the paper, to-morrow it will all beexplained away, and a laugh raised at the clever sceop. So it is with the Commander-in-Chief of the army. The only important part he has, as yet, taken in the war has leen to give evidence before a committee of enquiry as to whether he did or did not say some- ! thing about the quality of the beef sent out to the ! army. What is known as " yellow journalism" is to blame for all this silly work. It was the un- scrupulous work of the Democratic press that brought on the war, and it is its unpatriotic policy that makes the end seem so far away. In consNuence of the fault-finding attitude taken by the Democratic press the secretery of War Mr. Alger, has been asked to resign and a Sir. Root has been appointed to fill his place. I have no doubt Mr. Alger did his work as well as it was I possible under the circumstances. He was charged with appointing incapables. Now, he has left the ! office he shows that ninety per cent of the officers ! of the volunteers were appointed by the State Governors from which the volunteer;regiments were drawn. It seems to me that there is a lack of centraliza- tion. Poe Federal system is all very well in theory but it does not work out well. Responsibility is spread all over the States, As a consequence the friends of those who are in power, and incapables of all sorts are placed in positions they are utterly unable to fill. Worse than this, the party system of running politics has in the United States ran wild. Every man shouts and blazons forth his patriotism, but he always has his eye on the main point. What is there in it ? It is all right, and well to shout for liberty and democracy if one knows the true meaning of these words, but when a low notion of what these watch- words mean, leads to the utter destruction of all respect for the dignity that must attend respon- sibility it is time then to pause. It seems to me that with all the boasted perfection of her educa- tional system the schools of America fail to turn out the class of men and women who can give strength and permanency to a nation. It has been often said, and I must repeat it,the almightly dollar stands before everything. The nation's welfare, nay, the domestic altar all is sacrificed to the wor- ship of this metal god. In San Francisco we are now raising tunde to make a great demonstration on the return of the volunteers from the seat of war. There is to be such a turn out as the city never saw before. The main street is to be one great show of electric lights and flags, and I suppose the girls will do as they did with Hobson, of Merrimac fame, gush and kiss to their hearts' content:During this war the world has seen such wonders an it neversaw before. The finest ships of war, the bravest admirals, the most far-seeing and cutest of generals, and the most daring fire eaters have made up their rank and file. With all this splendid showing the goods remain undelivered, for which ..D1,000,000 were paid. If it were allowable to criticize one might point out that a little calm modesty would do some good. Lass shouting and talking about liberty and the Great American Republic might give some chance for common sense to have an innings. ! There seems something strange in the way that !men holding high positions, and claiming to be so very meek and mild long and seek for popular ap- plause. I cannot imagine a British Admiral mak- ing an imitation royal progress such as Dewey is now doing. Nor yet can I think that European Colonels and and Captains would write books, or make platform speechesior yet submit to the persis- tent interviewer such insubordinate or unpatriotic! statements as are now being spread abroad. It seems to be generally accepted here that the ! above kind of work, coupled with the literature the anti-annexationists are scattering abroad is doing much to lengthen out the war. However this may I be the party politicians are anxious to bring it to a close. Of course this is the expressed wish, from a ! patriotic paint of view, of the two parties I My own opinion is that the democrats would like the war to continue until near the coming election hoping to make capital out of it. The republicans on the other hand are betray- ing great anxiety to make an end of it. They openly ! confess that if the war is still in progress next year! and the end not in sight there will be a general republican slump To end the "unfortunate war" is a measure of the highest party importance, and party ietereets must be looked to whatever may be the fate of the Philipines. The nation does not speak with an unanimous voice, there can be no doubt of that. It cannot, it has no data upon which to form an opinion. The only way open at present form the republican point of view is to call Congress 'together and "assure the Filipinos that we have no I desire save to give them self-government under the American flag which may go further than an over- whelming force or soldiery to bring the conflict to an end." The Cuban an 1 Porto Rican matters are even of

more importance than are those of the Philipinos. These places are either annexed, or they are terri- tories. However this may be they are being governed under their old laws,and contract labours, or, in plain English, slave labour is the rule there under the liberty loving Stars and Stripes. In closing, may I refer to a matter of some little importance to you Britishers. I often see articles in the English papers I receive, which lead 1113 to think that the manufacturers of-the tight little is- land are doing wrong in making little of American competition. A recent jotting in the" Examiner," in which the writer tried to make the situation somewhat ridiculous by depreciating American manufacturers, is a case in point. I enclose a cut- ting taken from" Industrial Cuba," a book just out from the bands of Mr Porter, United States special commissioner, and I should be glad if you would print it for the benefit of your readers. The cutting alluded to says :—ln his concluding words on Commerce, Mr Porter writes: "Referring to the fact that the railways of Cuba under English control have had their machinery from the United States, the manager of the English railway in Cuba only as recently as October, 1898, informed tne author that they had not only purchased of the United States in the past, but that they intended getting all their railway supplies for the future from the same source. Surely no higher tribute could be paid to the manufacturers of our country than this from an Englishman, whose people for hundreds of years have led all competitors in the industrial manufacturing of the world. And this is but a step in the giant strides of commercial pro- gress the United States will make in Cuba under the encouragimg influences of a reasonable tariff, the abolition of all discrimination, the assurance of a stable government,and that proximity which makes Cuba one of us in temper, in trade and in territor y."

THE COUNTY BOROUGH QUESTION.

REPORT BY. ILIE TOWN CLERK.

The Town *Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) nas pre- sented a carefully prepared report on the Lccal Government Act of 1888. It will be remembered that he was instructed to de so in order to assist the Council in arriving at a conclusion on the question of the town becoming a County Borough in conse- quence of the treatment which has been meted out to Warrington by the Lancashire County Council. The report was laid before the General Purposes Committee at the last meeting, and after considera- tion the 6nbjtct was adjourned for further discus- sion.

===== 0002601_18990825_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TOWN CLERK'S REFORT

In his report the TowN CLERK says the Local Govern- meat Act of 1888 added to the complex differences which existed b-ftween various classes of boroughs by drawing an anomalous dislinction without regard to area or rateable value between boroughs that had not less than 50,000 inhabitants and those that bad. Certain other boroughs which were counties in themselves also became county boroughs, of which several had at the time a less population than Warrington. By the 1881 Census the population of Warrington was 41,452. With the view of having Warrington made a county borough a poll ot the inhabitants was taken on the 13 h May, 1888, by which it was ascertained that the population was 47,325. The President of the Local Government Board, however, declined to include Warrington in the list of County Boroughs. Other distinctions are made in the Act between various classes of boroughs, and the relations to the County Council depend, for instance, upon whether a borough has or has net a separate Court of Quarter Sessions, a Commission of the Peace, its own Police, or a population of more than 10,000. Boroughs are again differently affected with regard to main roads, weights and measures, and other matters but the observations with regard to the distinction be- tween non-county boroughs and County Councils will be confined to thi3 circumstances and conditions affect- ing Warrington. The county rate is levied by precept on the overseers, and is collected with and as part of the poor rate. The County Council have power to bor- row on the security of tteir funds and revenues, and thus the rateable value of the borough is twice mort- gaged, and the credit of the borough is made available for purposes which may not benefit the borough.

CONSTITUTION OF NEW COUNTY BOROUGHS:

The Act provides that the council of a borough may make a representation to the Local Government Board "That it is desirable to constitute any borough having a population of not less tban 50,000 into a csunty borough." The Board unless for special reasons they think that the representation ought not to be entertained shall cause a local inquiry to be held and may make the order. The order is provisional only, and has to be con- firmel by Parliament. The Bill to confirm the order may be opposed. Four boroughs, Oxford, Grimsby, Newport (Mon), and Bournemouth have become county boroughs under orders of the Local Government Board.

ELECTORAL REPRE9ENTA.TION. The number of County Councillors on the Lancashire County Council was originally 105. Of this num- ber 20 are elected from the non-county boroughs within tke county. Borough Councils are not represented on :he County Council. Warrington is divided into three electoral divisions' each of which elects a member triennially. Warringionhad three mem- bers assigned to it on the census of 1881 when the popu- lation was 41,452. The Local Government Board have power to make an Order to alter the electoral divisions of a borough, so as to give due representation to an in- creased urban population. A County Borough has no representative on the County Council.

THE FINANCIAL POSITION.

After explaining tare Imperial contribution to County Councils and County Boroughs in detail, the Town Clerk proceeds to state that before any reliable estimate could be formed of the financial position of Warrington on becoming a Coun,y Borough it would be necessary to obtain from the Commissioners of Inland Revenue a cer- tificate of the amount of the local taxation licenses col- lected in the borough. It would further be necessary to ascertain the proportinn of the estate duty grant, which is apportioned on the basis of the discontinued grants, and the share of the customs and excise duties, the distribution of which is providei for by the Local Taxation Customs and Excise Act, 1890, the Police Act, 1890, and the Technical Instruction Act, 1891. To ar- rive at an adjustment it would be necessary to ascertain the contributions to be paid by the borough on the cessa- tion of its liability to contribute towards the expenditure of the co3nty,:and the: contribution to be paid by the county on the cessation of its liabaity to incur expense wholly or partly on behalf of the borough, and, further, the adjustment would embrace the property, debts and liabilities. The late Clerk to the County Council, when applied to in 1894, stated that it was very imptobale that the Council would prepare a claim before Warring- ton was constituted a county borough, and " that cir- cumstances have very much altered since 1891, and that the claim by the county will b 3 made in accordance with suah changed circumstances."

===== 0002601_18990825_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
POLICE.

The report deals fully with the question of rates and loans, and in referring to the town's position respecting police, the Town Clerk states that, Warrington, having its own separate Commission of the Peace, and as its own police pays one-half cost out of the Borough Rate, and receives the other half from the County Council on the Certificate of the Secretary of State. As a County Borough it would deduct one-half of the cost from the amount received from the local taxation account. The County:Police have no authority within the borough, but they may execute their processes within the bossugh without having to get them backed by a Borough Justice.

===== 0002601_18990825_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
MAIN ROADS.

The Borough is in an unf)rtunate and at the same. time most unfair fposition with regard to main roads With the exception of the short lengths of main roads which were taken into the borough by the recent extensions of its boundaries there are no main roads, notwithstanding the fact that some of its principle streets are the main highways between two important counties. The Act of 1888 threw upon the County the cost of repairing all main roads and the Borough is repaid the cost of repairing the main roads taken into the Borough. The Borough has to contribute to the repair of the rural main roads in the county. The County Council has in effect stated that they will not de- elare any roads in the taiga Boroughs to be main roads, although they have create.l over 100 miles of new main roads in the County since the act was passed, and the non-County Boroughs have to contribute to the cost The County has go: rd of its obligations to the Boroughs by granting a share of the residue of the local taxation account for the crainteaance of what the county terms " secondary roads," but the grant is made on terms that the roads shall cease to be secondary roads in the event of any borough becoming a county borough. The cost of maintaining the two county roads, Wilderspool Causeway and Latchford Causeway,has been commuted, and the. cest of commuting the main roads taken into the borough from the Cheshire County is limited by the extension order of 1896, as follows : —"ln the event of the Borough of Warrington becoming a county borough the liab:lities in regard to main roads within the Cheshire added area,the Latchford Causeway and Black Lion Bridge shall be excluded from any claim for com- pensation by the Corporation in respect thereof. Pro- vided, however, that the Corporation shall be entitled to receive, cr have credit given to them for a capital sum based on the annual average expenditure for the last three complete precsding years on the said last mentioned main roads, Latchford elauseway and Black Lion Bridge respectively, and calculated at the number of years (not exceeding in the whole ten years) during which the County Council of the County Palatine of Lancaster, have repaired or c)ntributed to the repair of the said main roads, the Latchford Oauseway and Black Li In Bridge respectively."

COUNTY AND HUNDIIED BRIDGES.

The liability to maintain the bridges- within the Borough now rests upon the County C ouncil, but would be transferred to Warrington upon becoming a county borough. Where P. bridge became a county bridge before 1836 the highway tor 300 feet on each side of the bridge was repairable by the county. The County Council may restrict or prevent the use of locomotives upon the bridgcs within the borough.

===== 0002601_18990825_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION AND TECHNICAL INSIMUCTION. _

The County Council possess certain powers of inter- ference with education within boroughs. The Tt chnical Instruction Act of 1889 empowers the County Council to act within non-country boroughs,and those boroughs are not exempted from contribution to the county rate raised for the purpose. The rate to be raised is not to exceed id, but in non-county boroughs the rate may be 2d,ld to be levied by the borough and id by the county. The Local Taxation Act of 1890 gave the residue of a large Parliamentary grant entirely to the councils of county and county boroughs, authorising them to apply it either in the same way as the estate duty grant or by contributions to technical and manual education. The Act authorises the County Council to pay over to the non-county Loroughs part of the sum to be devoted to technical and manual instruction, but there is nothing in the Ant to compel the county to give to the borough any part or an adequate part of the grant. If the borough becomes a County Borough the amount it would be entitled to on the ratio of rateable values would be received from the local taxation account.

The appeal referred to is as follows :—" To the people of Great Britain ! —We, ee Kite of the Netherienis are related to you, the people of Great Britain,by many ties. Our origin was the same ; our nietory has been similar ; our tralitions. customs and institutioue are closely alliei. This rehttion,hip we regard as an honour, but also as entitling us to the privilege of expressing to you that which the de le conviction of our hearts and minds alike neoet forcibly impel us to express. We fear that many of you are on the verge of violating the rights of the people of the South African Republic, a branch of cur commoa stock. They may be weak in number, but they are strong in those virtues, which are counted hightest by all Anglo-Sax .ns : they eave courage, love of liberty, self-reliance an:: a profoun .ly religious spirit. Appealing to your deep sense 'if justice and to your broad generosity we cry out to you : Desist from aay attempt of money ;makers or et ite.stnen to hinder the gradual and national development of this people's own nature or genius, trying to m tke them suej :et to your oven will. Desist from creating and seizing every opportun- ity and pretext for interfering with their private aff sirs, for no better reaeon t'ian that they manage those matters in a way, which to you may net appear reasonable or desirable. We appeal to you to lee this people follow a course of natural growth, in accordance with the aphis of the age, which, while furthering progress, both ne tterial and spiritual, con- demns violent and arbitrary interference as mere likely to retard than to promote their gradual development. This we ask of you most earneely, not only because any other line of action neuet needs lead to violence, to bloodshed and te fratricide, bnt also as any such action would he an outrage on justice, cast a blet on the lair name of British equity,deal a blow at British intelligence and make a mock of British generosity."

THE NEW TELEPTIONE ACT (199) AND WAY-LEAVES.

Sir,-1 word of warning,app,Ars very necessary to local authcrities in conn(cPion with the grants of under-

ground and other telephooeway-leaves which ,according to press report, are cotistaw ly being made. Town . Councils and other local authorities shoall make them- selves thoroughly acquaitrmd with the provisions of the new Telephone Act before entertaining any further application for way-leave, or they may hereafter fiad that they have unwi tingly sacrificed the interests of their rarepayers, and tied the hands of their successors in office. The new Act provides that in the event of any local authority determining hereafter to start or sanction a competitive telephone exchange within its area (which the new Act empowers it to no) any way-leaves pre- viously granted will be irrevocable dur:nz the term for which the new exchaagOslicensed by the Post Office,and this in spite of aay azreemetit to terminate the way- leaves at six months or other notice which may have been entered into. Common sense, therefore, requires that way-leave grants should at once cease, or at least be subject to entirely nw conditions conceived in the interests of the local telephone users old ratepayers, whose street; it i; proposed to appropriate. It cannot be too strongly stated that competition or redaction of rats e in the future, will be rendered difficult, if not im- possible, by throwing away at the presnit criticel moment, the only weapon capable of b.nag effectively wielded in the interests of the pubic. Any claim to exercise tee way—leave powers of the Postmaster-General. even when apparently supported by &cements iffelcd by tile Post Office, should tie clotely scrutinised and tested by reference to the Telegraph Acts, as the Postmister-General can only delegate his players subject to the consent of the Local Authoii ies concerned. This is made quite plain by Section sof the Telegraph Act of 1892. 'Go It is mtnresting to n, te 'hat some Local Authorities are iully alive to the importance r,f this subject since, in view of the new Act, blth full and Bradfotel town Councils have given notice to terminate their grants of way7leaves.-1 ale, Sir, yours etc., A. tLAIff FOR THE LOSS OF AN tYE

AT WAR RINUTON.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday morning, before his Honour Judge Wynne Floulkes an important claim was made under the Wo.kmen'e Compensation Act. The applicant was Patrick Tannion, a labourer living at 126, Anne-street, Sankey Bridges, and the respondents were Messrs. Joseph Cro.-tield andSou;,€,3ap re.annfecturers, Bank Quay. In his request the applicant alleged that on January 2Sth of this year, be was steaming caustic pipes at the works, when owing to a defective tap in the pipe, he received an injury to his right eye, which destroyed the si.ht. He was under medieal treatment fer twelve weeks, during which time he received 12- 61 per we 4( from the respondents. He was now employed as a labourer, and earned 178 id per week. He claimed from the respondents 12s 6 1 per week tor life. Mr. Forshaw appeared for the applicant, and Mr. Payne, of Payne, Galloway and Payne, Manchester, for the respondents. Mr. Forshaw was proceeding to detail the facts of the case, when His Honour said the ease had been adjourned from the last court to see if the parties could come to terms. He wished to know whether that hack been done. Mr. Forshaw said they had endeavoured to effect a settlement; but the amount offered was sadly too His Hoaour asked Mr. Payne why an answer to the request had not been filed. Mr. Payne said they had been making endeavours to come to terms. His Honour remarked that the negotiations need not have prevented the filing of the answer. Mr. Payne said the answer of Mr. For-haw to their last offer Was not delivera to him for the filing of the answer. His Honour replied thAt he did not regard that as a suffic;ent reason. Mr. Payne said thzt at that time the firm was not act- in v under legal advice. His Honour : I am afraid it is not an excuse. The rule noon the point is very explicit. Unless you can ' show good vegan Id ) no teel disposed to allow you to proceed. The Act was intended to prevent litigation. The answer should have been filed within five days of the receipt of the summons. Mr. Payne submitted that th 3, contemplation that parties would arrive at a, settlement was a reasonable excuse because had they done so the filing of the answer would have been needless. Both parties anticipated a settlement. His Honour : I don't feel disposed to allow this to b 3 litigated. I must take the allegation of the plaintiff in his request to be comet and admitted by the re- spondents. Unless tte parties can come ti terms in the course of the morning, I most make an order for 12s 6J weekly for 1 fe. In answer to Mr. Forshaw, His Honour said that costs would follow.

ELECTRIC LIGHT IN ST. HELENS CHURCHES.

The value of the electric light as an illu mina at fo churches and large public meeting-rooms has been fully recognlsed in St. Helens, where the Town gall and five of the largest places of worship are now lighted by elec- tricity. The light was used in Lowe House—the mother Catholic Church of the district—ca Sunday night, and although the installation was not quite complete, the church was beautifully lighted.

MEETING OF THE MAATIN3CEOFT PARISH COUNCIL.—A special meeting oi this Council was held on Monday evening in the schoolroom. Martinscroft, for the pur- pose of filling up the vacancy caused by the death of Mr. William Massey, overseer, and also vice-chairman of the Council. Mr. William Houghton presided, and there was a good attendance. Flattering references were made to the services of the deceased, and a vote of sympathy was passed with the relatives in their sad bereavement, the Chairman observing that Mr. Massey had held the office of vice-chairman of the Council since its formation. He also took a great interest in the pro- vision of the recreation ground, great improvements having been carried out under his supervision. Mr. David Massey was electf d to fill the vacancy on the Council, and Mr. Nicholas Massey was electocl overseer. Both these gentlemen are brother 3 of the deceased. S7DDEN DEATH OF A. WOOLSTON FARMER.—The death occurvd under painfully sudden circumstances of Mr. William Wainwright, of Woolston. It appears that the , deceased, who was 69 years ot age, rose abcut five o'clock on Saturday merning, when he appeared to be in his usual health, and!partook of a hearty breakfast. About ten o'clock he went into a field adjoining his farm- yard to do some mowing. Threequarters of an hour afterwards he was seen by Mary Ellen Platt hurrying towards his house. When near the yard gate he fell to the ground, and on a woman named Owen going to him she found that he was dead. The sad occurrence has caused quite a Sensation in the discrict, where deceased had resided for a number of years. He had suffered from heart disease during the past 211 years, and had been medically attended by Des. Fox and Sherratt, of Warrington, but, since March last he had not been under medical treatment. The attack, which resulted in his death is attributei to the excessive heat. The Coroner did not deem an inquest necessary.

WARRINGTON.

MEETINGS OF SPIRITUALISTS.

Mrs. Fielding, of Hollinwood, occupied the plat- form at the Spiritualist Hall on Sunday, and in the evening gave an address on "In His steps I will dare to follow." In the afternoon she conducted a circle, and at both meetings gave clairvoyance. On Mon- day evening, Miss Chadderton, a friend of Mrs. Fieldings, paid Warrington a visit, and in the place of the, lady occupied the evening with clairvoyance and psychometry DEATH AND FUNERAL OF Mn. THO MIS EAUDE

Many will regret to hear of the death of Mr Thomas Eaude (the only surviving son of the late Mr. Thomas Eaude, for many years organist at the Warrington Parish Church), which occurred at his residence, Bruche House, Manchester-road, on Saturday. The decemed, who was well known and much respected in social circles in Warrington, was at Llandudno on Saturday week. but on the follow- ing day, while sitting in his house, he was s,-izid with paralysis. He was attended by Dr. Joseph, but the patient never spoke again, neither did he regain consciousness. Therematns of the deceased, who was 48 years of age, and was a coal merchant were interred in the Cemetery on Wednesday.

THE FIRE BRIGADE DISPUTE.-APPLICATIONS FOR

RE-INSTATEMHNT.

At a meeting of the Fire Engine Committee held on Monday evening, Councillor Smethurst presid- ing, the whole of. the old fire brigade with the ex- cqotion of two, applied to be re-instated, and after considaration the committee decided to accept the application on the old terms, and on condition that a three months' notice on either side should ter- minate the engagement. The men left with the committee a statement of their grievances, and the Chairman promised it would receive attention at a future meeting. Since the termination of the fire- teens notice,th &committee have had at their disposal the services of the Wilderspool Brigade and of the firemen engaged at various works in the town. By the re-instatement of the old brigade that arrange-

_ ment is no longer necessary. The committee hive appointed a few additional firemen.

THE NEW ALLAN LINER.

The Twin-Screw steamer "Bavarian," 10,200 tons gross, built by Me,srs. Danny for the Allan Line, starts on her initial voyage from Liverpool to Que- bec and Montreal, on Thursday next, the 24,-,h inst. ILuxurious accommodation is provided for 160 first class passengers, while the quarters devoted to the 1150 second diva and 200 third class passengers, mark a big advance on anything yet provided in the Canadian Trade for these classes of voyages. Even steerage passengers have smoke room, ladies saloon, shower baths, B:c., provided for them, and this improved catering to the comfort of steerage voyagers will be much appreciated. Should occa- sion require, 1,000 3rd class passengers can be ac- commodated. In addition to luxurious passenger quarters the" Bavarian " has a large refrigerating space for perishable goods, and as on her trial trip she averaged 17.95 knots, she will be the speediest as she is the largest vessel employed in the Canadian trade.

THE CHIEF CONSTABLE'S ANNUAL REPORT.

The following is the report,of the Chief Constaeele sub- mitted to the Licensing Committee :—Chief Constable's Offices, August 20th, 1899. To the Worshipful the Jus- tices assembled at the Annual Licensing Meeting. Gen- tlemen,-1 have the honour to submit for your informa- tion any fourth annual report relative to the business of the Brewster Sessions. The period dealt with is the year ended July 31st, 1899. The population of .the borough a. extended, was 55,068, according to the census of 1891. The present estenated population i 563,000. There are in the borough 87 alehouses' 40 beertemses, me 85 off-licenses (including thoee for the sale of wine or spirits), total 212, a proportion of one license to every 297.17 of the estimated population: During the year one beerseller was fined for b jog drunk upon his licensed premises. The licensed houses have been fre- queotly visited by the police for purposes of sueervisioci, and althmeh there has been but one proeecution, several license holders have been cautioned for irregularities not amounting to wilful breaches of the law. Licenses have been transferred to ten licensed viotuallere, five beerhouse-keepers, and ten off- license holders, making a total of 25. Last year the corresponding total was 23. Twentv-iix alehouses, seven beerhouses, and eleven other public rooms are licensed for music and dancing. No professional performers are employed at the al-houses or beerh )(is as. During the year 418 charges of drunkenness have been heard by the magistrates, 271 being against males and 147 against females. In 385 cases there were convictions, and 33 ch,a egos were dismissed. Last year the prosecu- tions numbered 276. Of the persons t hareed this year 295 were resident in the borough, and 122 were non- resi,tent, mostly of the vagrant class. It will be ob- served that the figures in the present return show a con- siderable increase in the number of charges of drunken- ness when compared with the corresponding totals for the two previous years, during which the number of drunken charges were fewer than in any year recorded in our police registers; P; is proverbially difficult to eccount accurately for the fluctuations of such statie- ticcebut in industrial centres a flourishing state of trade, such as has existed in Warrington during the past year, almost invariably has a tendency to swell the num- ber of charges of drunkenness. Evensive building operations in the town and neighbourhood have, moreover, attracted large numbers of persons to the borough, and the charees of drunkenness amongst this class had been very numerous indeed. Again, it is to be noted that of the total, 418, 84 charges represent only 32 persons proceeded against, inasmuch as two persons were convicted six times each, one person five', times, two persons four times, five persons three times, and 22 persons twice each during the year. The Inebri- ates Act came into force on the Ist January last, but is still inoperative, as no provision has yet been made in this district for ;he reception and detention of habitual drunkards under this statute. A movement is, however, on foot which will, I understand, remedy this deficiency in the near future. The little Horse Shoe alehouse was closed we the 10th of October, the license lapsing in par- tial consideration of the grant of an alehouse license to the Hawthorn Hotel, Orford-lane, which was opened as an alehouse on the 11th Oetober, having previously been a beerhouse. The beerhouse formerly known as the Blacksmiths Arms was opened as an alehouse, under the sign of the Great Central Hotel, on the sth of June On the same day the Harp and Crown beerhouse was closed, the license lapsing as part consideration of the full !teepee granted to the Great Central Hotel. The Causeway Hotel opened as an alehouse nn September 29th, having formerly been a beerhouse known as the Pleasure Boathouse. In consideration of the grant to the last-named premises, two beerhousee had ben closed prior to the last Brewster Sessions. At this session, three a pplicatiens for new licenses will be made." Table I, appended to the report gives the number of licensed victuallers, beerhouse keepers,shopkeepers(who have licenses) and the nember of persons charged with drunkenness since the year 1890. It appears that the number of licensed victuallers is exactly tho same as in 1890, hut the nemberof beerhoueekeepers has decreased from 48 in 1890 to 40 in 1899. The number of limner) shopkeepers on the other hand has increased from 70 in 1890 to 85 this year. In 1890 the number of persons charged with drunkenness was 806 whereas there were 418 this year. _ _ . - .

ACCIDENT TO THE LORD MAYOR OF LIVERPOOL.

The Lord Mayor of Liverpool had a severe accident on Wednesday at Ambloside, where he is spending his holi- days. While descending a steep hill he lost la's footing and fell with some violence a considerable distance. Fortunately some members of his family were with him, and two doctors were promptly in attendance. They found that though no bones were broken his lordship was suffering from shock and severe concussion. A tele- gram received in Liverpool on Thursday reported a rest- less night.

A Capetown correspondent telegraphs :—I learn from an excellent, though unofficial, source that the com- munication sent by the Transvaal Government to the High C nnmissiener contains the following proposals : A five years' franchise. A share in the election of the President. Increasld representation for the aaldfielde to the ex- tent, probably, of eight now seats. Other questions to be submitted to arbitration, but not to that of a foreign Power. Great Britain to agree not to use her prPB6nt interfer- ence as a precedent. Great Britain to agree to relinquish her euzeraiaty rights. STARTLING SUBSIDENCE AT NORTHWICH.

On Wednesday night a peculiar and somewhat serious subsidence occurred in Leicester-street, Northwich, one O f the m)t notorious sinking acres in the Cheshire salt district. Mrs. Kennerley, wife of a labourer, was iron- ing in a cottage which is in a partially wrecked condi- tion owing to sinkage wise/111We heard rumbling sounds. Cracks in the fi3or assumed the proportions of large fissures, and suddenly two-thirds of the floor and a por tion of an inner wall collapsed with a crash. Mr Kennerley had a narrow escape, and her son was caugh by the falling debris and bruised on the legs. The cella of the house was recently filled in, but the bottom mus have sunk, for thick flags and brickwork fell a distanc of some feet. A portion of the kitchen and pantry wal gave way,while large cracks appear in the outer wall. A strange part of the affair is that with the customary nlifference of Noehwich people, the tenants continue t ioccu py the derelict house. THE DISTURBA.NCES IN PARIS. M. GUERIN STILL HOLDING our.
