NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] Contrary to expectation the Newton Horticul- tural Society were favoured with exceptionally fine weather on the occasion of their annual show. The morning opened dull and tareatening,and although rain fell early on putting the anticipations of the committee below zero, the ominous looking clouds broke and finally cleared away. Ultimately the sun struggled througn and the remainder of the day was beautifully fine and warm. The surround- ings of the show were naturally pleasant, and charming, and the visitors had a most enjoyable time of it. Of the show itself little may be said, but if appearances go for anything, it is evident that its popularity is on the wane. Why that is so, I cannot say, because there are quite sufficient gentry in the neighbourhood to get together an ex- cellent exhibition. The number of entries was much below the average, but the judges stated that the quality of the exhibits was superior to those of other years. Still it is not enOuraging that the competition should be confined to three or four ex- hibitors.

The narrow escape from drowning which a little girl had last week brings to my mind the exceed- ingly dangerous condition of tie boundaries of the brook in which she almost lost her lite. At the point in question the brook is very deep, and it is quite a common occurrence for numbers of child- ren to be seen playing on the low wall abetting the brook on each side of the roadway. Not only so, but quite young lads may be seen almost any time sitting on the wall fishing and even climbing over and walking along the sluices below the wall anci just above the water, How it has come about that the place has been so free from accidents. I cannot conceive, but I suppose it is more providential than otherwise. I think the owners of the property should be compelled to make some other provision for ensuring the safety of the children, by either building the walls higher or putting an iron fence along the coping of each wall.

The residents in Earle-street will not raise a single objection to the postponement of the question of erecting a urinal between the Co-operative Bociety's shoo, and the Griffin Hotel,and they will be pleased that the London and North-Western Railway Com- pany, the owners of the property have raised an objection to the urinal. It is very kind of the Dis- trict Council to propose the erection of such necessary places of convenience, but it is rather too bad to propose to put such places immediately opposite, and in close proximity to residental property.

IMr. Gilmour still seemed to have the cheap I notoriety fit upon him, and evidently wishes to I pose as the champion ot progress. In his endeavour Ito get the Council to bcok up his suggestion for the appointment of four additional County magis- trates, he was not successful, and if one might judge by the voting, the gentlemen he named, were not desirous of entering the ranks of the great un- paid, at least not by the method suggested by Mr. Gilmour. They prefer that the selection shall be made by the County Bench, which really is the proper channel through which such appointments should emanate.

Fisheries, and Mersey and Irwell (Rivers Pollution Pre Tendon). RIVERS POLLUTION. The County Council have concurrent powers within the borough under the Rivers Pollution Prevention Act, 1876. The County Council also claim to have concurrent authority under the We'ghts and Measures Act, the Sale of Food and Drugs Act, and under the Act of 1888 with respect to the duties of their Medical Officer of Health. None of such powers can be exercised in a County Borough, aud speaking generally, without considering the prestige and impor'ance of county over a ncn- county blrouz,h, there is no doubt that the former pos- sesses complete independerre and autonomy. • QUOITS. LATEST NEWS. THROWING A MISSIVE TO THE QUEEN. A FOREIGNER'S MISTAKE . The Queen was approaching the gates of the palace at Osborne on Wednesday on returning from her evening drive, when a respectively-Aress-d man standing at the side of the roadway threw a letter into the carriage as it passed. Her Majesty, with whom was riding one of the ladies of the Court, seemed surprised but betrayed no alarm. Th 3 envelope, a small one, on being opened, was found to contain a request that the Queen would use her influence to secure for the writer a new trial of a case which he recently lost in the Eaglish Law Court:, Supt. Fraser,of the Royal Household Police, and a mem- ber of tt-e Scotland Yard Detective Force, quickly went in search of the man and found him in the neignbeur- hood of Osborne at a house where he had lodged Vale previous night. He was wearing a silk bat and frock coat at the time he threw the lEtter into the carriage, and there appeared no reason to regard him with sus- picion. He was found upon inquiry to be a German, who lives in Landen. He states that he did nut know he was breaking the law, as on the Continent it is com- mon to tarow MiS3iVOS into Royal carriages. He gave a correct name and'address, and after inquiries were made, which proved satisfactory, he was liberated. Considerable alarm was caused at first by an exag- gerated rumour that the envelope contained an explo dive substance, a rumour which happily proved to be without foundation.
